Below is the **parameterized-help instruction module draft**, followed by the **design-doc addition** and **ADR draft** I recommend for `MVR-GIS/reproducibleai` so the package itself records this capability.

## Instruction module draft

````markdown name=parameterized-help.md
# Parameterized Help

## Purpose
This module governs how a `golem`-based Shiny app implements contextual help as structured, reusable, package-managed data rather than as ad hoc prose scattered through UI and server code.

The goal is to create a contextual help system that is:
- maintainable
- composable
- testable
- aligned with implemented app behavior
- reusable across help surfaces such as tabs, plots, tables, and input controls

This module is about:
- help-data architecture
- help-data storage conventions
- stable help IDs
- help composition and rendering
- help-surface roles
- help-specific CSS requirements
- help maintenance and drift control

This module is not about:
- general Shiny UI architecture
- app-specific domain content beyond help-system structure
- generic CSS unrelated to help surfaces
- package-wide governance outside the help system

## When to use
Use this module when:
- the repository contains a `golem`-based Shiny app developed as an R package
- the app needs contextual help across multiple surfaces
- help content should be reusable and centrally managed
- the team wants the chat session to implement and maintain a structured help system rather than writing one-off inline help text

This module is especially appropriate when:
- help appears in popovers, sidebars, tables, plots, or tab-level guidance
- the app exposes many derived variables or technical outputs
- the help system must stay aligned with changing package functionality over time

## Assumptions
This module assumes:

- the app is built with `golem`
- the app is an R package
- help content is stored as package data
- runtime helper functions are provided by `reproducibleai` and imported into the app package rather than copied into the app repository by default
- user-facing contextual help is part of the app’s functional interface and should be treated as a maintained system, not as incidental prose

## Scope and boundaries
This module governs:
- the structure of help data
- the storage and generation of help data
- the use of stable IDs as the contract between help content and UI references
- the composition of help records into UI surfaces
- the distinction between overview help and granular help
- help-specific CSS support for complex popovers
- integrity checking and drift review

This module does not govern:
- the app’s domain logic
- the overall navigation structure of the app
- non-help-related CSS or theming
- general package governance
- broad user documentation such as README or pkgdown articles except where those docs must explain the help framework itself

## Required architecture

### 1. Help content must be structured data
Contextual help must be managed as structured data rather than inline prose in UI/server code.

Required fields:
- `id`
- `title`
- `summary`
- `detail`

These fields form the canonical help-data contract.

### 2. Help data must be created in `data-raw/`
Author and maintain help content in:

- `data-raw/create_help_data.R`

This script should generate the canonical help dataset.

### 3. Help data must be stored as package data
Persist help data as package data in:

- `data/help_data.rda`

Document the dataset in:

- `R/help_data.R`

### 4. Help IDs are the contract
Each help record must have a stable `id`.

UI and server code must reference help by ID rather than embedding duplicated prose.

IDs should be:
- stable over time
- semantically meaningful
- specific enough to support reuse
- updated deliberately during refactors

### 5. Help must be composable
The help system must support composing multiple help records into a single help surface.

Use:
- overview records for surfaces such as tabs, plots, and tables
- granular records for variables, quantities, metrics, methods, and concepts

Do not duplicate the same explanation across multiple surfaces when composition can be used instead.

### 6. Rendering should use reusable helpers
Use `reproducibleai` runtime helpers for composing and rendering help.

Preferred pattern:
- `reproducibleai::compose_help_items()`
- `reproducibleai::render_help_items()`

or imported equivalents in the app package namespace.

Do not copy these helpers into app repositories unless there is a deliberate reason to fork or customize behavior.

### 7. Help surfaces must have distinct roles
Different help surfaces should serve different purposes.

Recommended roles:
- getting-started / intro help: workflow orientation
- tab/sidebar help: decision framing and what to inspect
- plot help: what the plot shows and how to interpret it
- table help: what the table contains and how to use it
- granular variable help: definitions, formulas, units, and interpretation

Do not make every help surface a full technical essay.

### 8. Help popovers must support long-form content
If composed help is displayed in popovers or similar constrained containers, the app must include help-specific CSS that supports:
- wider popovers
- scrollable popover bodies
- readable long-form content

Help content must remain usable when multiple records are composed into one UI surface.

### 9. Formula rendering should be conservative
When mathematical notation is needed:
- prefer block MathJax
- avoid fragile inline math where possible
- keep formulas aligned with implemented code and units

### 10. Help must describe implemented behavior
Help text must describe what the app currently computes and displays.

Do not document:
- aspirational functionality
- intended future behavior
- domain formulas that differ from the implemented code without explicitly saying so

If implementation and theory differ, the help should describe the implementation clearly.

## Required conventions

### Help record categories
Use consistent categories of records such as:
- inputs
- intermediate or derived variables
- plot overview records
- table/scenario overview records
- tab/help-surface overview records
- reusable conceptual records

### Summary vs detail
Use:
- `summary` for concise orientation
- `detail` for fuller explanation

Do not collapse everything into `detail` unless the specific UI surface truly only needs one field.

### Naming and phrasing
Use consistent terminology for:
- units
- variable names
- adopted vs candidate outputs
- mass vs volume vs quantity
- method names

Prefer alignment with the app’s actual column names and outputs.

## Required chat behavior

### During implementation
When building or extending a help system, the chat must:
1. establish or verify the structured help-data architecture first
2. recommend or use `reproducibleai` scaffolding where appropriate
3. create help records as reusable data rather than inline prose
4. compose help records into surfaces rather than duplicating content
5. distinguish overview records from granular records

### During review and refinement
The chat must:
1. verify that referenced help IDs exist
2. identify missing records for newly exposed outputs or concepts
3. identify orphaned records that remain useful but unwired
4. identify overlap or redundancy across help surfaces
5. recommend concise improvements where help surfaces are doing the wrong job

### During maintenance
The chat must:
1. update help after code changes that affect displayed behavior, formulas, units, or output names
2. flag stale help after renames or refactors
3. propose paste-ready updates when drift is detected
4. keep help aligned with what the app currently renders and computes

## Maintenance triggers
Update help data and/or help composition when:
- a new variable, output, plot, or table surface is added
- a displayed field is renamed
- a formula or unit convention changes
- a help composition vector changes
- a plot begins showing different metrics than before
- a tab’s role changes
- a refactor introduces missing or stale IDs

## Integrity and drift checks
A parameterized help system should be maintained with explicit checks such as:
- referenced IDs exist in `help_data`
- IDs are unique
- required columns exist
- help composition reflects currently displayed outputs
- formulas, units, and descriptions match the implemented code

The chat should surface these checks during review work and provide paste-ready fixes when mismatches are found.

## Anti-patterns
Avoid the following:

### 1. Hard-coded help prose spread across the app
Do not scatter long help text directly through UI/server code when it should be part of structured help data.

### 2. Unstable or ad hoc IDs
Do not treat IDs casually. They are the contract between help content and UI references.

### 3. Duplicating the same explanation everywhere
Prefer composition and reusable records.

### 4. Treating all help surfaces as equivalent
Each surface should have a distinct role and level of detail.

### 5. Letting help drift from implementation
Do not leave formulas, units, names, or plot descriptions stale after code changes.

### 6. Using long popovers without CSS support
If help content is rich or composed, ensure the UI remains readable.

### 7. Copying reusable runtime helpers into each app by default
Prefer importing `reproducibleai` helpers unless there is an intentional reason not to.

## Completion rule
A parameterized-help task is not complete if the session has:
- added or renamed help IDs
- changed help architecture
- changed displayed outputs or formulas
- altered help composition
- introduced new plotted or tabulated metrics

without also:
- updating the relevant help data
- checking help-ID integrity
- surfacing paste-ready updates for the user

## Relationship to other modules
This module is intended to be used alongside:
- package-development modules
- `golem`-specific Shiny modules
- development-governance modules

This module governs the contextual-help system only. It should remain separate from broader governance and broader Shiny architecture modules.
````

## Recommended `dev/10_design.md` addition for `reproducibleai`

````markdown name=dev-10-design-parameterized-help.md
## Parameterized-help capability

### Purpose
`reproducibleai` standardizes a reusable pattern for contextual help in `golem`-based Shiny apps developed as R packages.

This capability is intended to help teams implement help systems that are:
- structured as package data
- composed from stable reusable records
- rendered through shared helper functions
- maintainable as app functionality evolves

### Scope
This capability governs:
- help-data architecture
- help-data storage and package-data conventions
- stable help IDs
- composition and rendering of contextual help
- help-specific CSS support
- integrity and drift review practices

It does not govern:
- app domain logic
- general Shiny architecture
- general package governance outside the help system

### Standard architecture
The package assumes a help-data workflow with:
- `data-raw/create_help_data.R` as the authoring script
- `data/help_data.rda` as the package dataset
- `R/help_data.R` as dataset documentation
- stable `id`, `title`, `summary`, and `detail` fields
- overview and granular help records composed into app surfaces

### Runtime helper model
The preferred model is for app packages to import reusable help helpers from `reproducibleai` rather than copying them into each app repository.

`reproducibleai` should provide the reusable runtime behavior, while app repositories should own:
- their help content
- their help composition choices
- their app-specific help wording

### Maintenance model
This capability assumes that help is part of the app’s maintained interface.

When app functionality changes, help should be reviewed for:
- missing IDs
- stale names
- stale formulas
- stale unit descriptions
- composition mismatches with current UI surfaces
````

---

## Recommended ADR draft for `reproducibleai`

````markdown name=adr-parameterized-help.md
# ADR-XXXX: Adopt parameterized help for `golem`-based Shiny apps as a `reproducibleai` capability

## Status
Accepted

## Context
The team has developed and validated a contextual-help pattern in a `golem`-based Shiny app in which help content is stored as structured package data, referenced by stable IDs, and composed into multiple UI surfaces through reusable rendering helpers.

This pattern proved useful for maintaining consistency across help surfaces, reducing duplication, and supporting drift review as app functionality evolved.

## Decision
`reproducibleai` will adopt parameterized contextual help for `golem`-based Shiny apps as a first-class package capability.

This capability will standardize:
- structured help data with required fields
- package-data storage conventions for help content
- stable IDs as the contract between help data and UI references
- reusable runtime helpers for composing and rendering help
- scaffolding support for help-system setup in app repositories
- maintenance expectations for integrity checks and drift review

The default model is that app repositories import reusable runtime helpers from `reproducibleai` rather than copying those helpers locally.

## Consequences
### Positive
- improves consistency and maintainability of contextual help systems
- reduces duplicated prose across app surfaces
- supports integrity checking and drift review
- makes the pattern reusable across multiple `golem` apps

### Tradeoffs
- introduces an opinionated help-system architecture
- requires deliberate maintenance of help data during app evolution
- assumes a package-based app structure and is not intended as a generic pattern for all Shiny contexts

## Notes
This capability is separate from the broader development-governance capability. Development governance covers how repositories maintain durable design and decision records; parameterized help covers how a Shiny app implements and maintains contextual help as part of its user interface.
````

See changes in commit fb1788f5bc00162c571e031d1454f600de9a845c and 902d12390ad57b0bf1c05edaae76005a1861275d. Proceeding with functions for parameterized-help is certainly easier, but I feel like we should address the more fundamental capability of development-governance first.  

## Copilot

Yes — I agree. The more fundamental next step is to define the **function surface for development-governance** first.

That sequencing is better because:

- parameterized-help will sit on top of the broader governance framework
- governance scaffolding is applicable across many repo types, not just Shiny apps
- it lets `reproducibleai` establish a coherent foundation before adding more specialized capability layers

So I recommend we now define:

> **What functions, if any, should `reproducibleai` provide to support the development-governance capability?**

---

# Direct recommendation

For development-governance, `reproducibleai` should provide a **small, high-value function surface** focused on:

1. **scaffolding** the `dev/` governance structure
2. **validating** that the expected structure exists
3. optionally **reporting** gaps in governance artifacts

It should **not** try to automate the substantive writing of plan/design/ADR content.  
Per your clarified workflow, that is the job of the **instruction modules and chat sessions**, which should provide paste-ready updates.

So the functions should support:
- setup
- verification
- consistency

not:
- content authorship

That is an important boundary.

---

# Recommended function surface for development-governance

## 1. `use_dev_governance()`
### Purpose
Scaffold the standard `dev/` governance structure into a repository.

### This should create
Required by default:
- `dev/05_plan.md`
- `dev/10_design.md`
- `dev/decisions/`
- `dev/instructions/`
- `dev/sessions/`

Optional:
- `dev/40_schemas.md`

### It should also
- create starter content/templates in the files
- avoid overwriting existing files unless explicitly requested
- message clearly about what was created vs already existed

### Why this is valuable
It standardizes the framework across repos and lowers setup friction.

---

## 2. `validate_dev_governance()`
### Purpose
Check whether a repository contains the expected governance artifacts.

### What it should check
Required:
- existence of `dev/`
- existence of required files/folders
- basic readability / non-empty template presence maybe

Optional checks:
- whether `dev/decisions/` exists
- whether `dev/instructions/` exists
- whether files are still untouched boilerplate
- whether optional `dev/40_schemas.md` is present

### Output
Should return a structured result, something like:
- `ok`
- `missing_required`
- `missing_optional`
- `notes`

And print a readable summary.

### Why this is valuable
This provides light verification without overengineering.

---

## 3. `use_instruction_entrypoint()` or fold into `use_dev_governance()`
### Purpose
Create the top-level `dev/instructions/CHAT_INSTRUCTIONS.md` entrypoint and perhaps minimal module/index scaffolding.

### Recommendation
This may be folded into `use_dev_governance()` rather than exposed separately, unless your package already benefits from separating instruction-related scaffolding.

Because `reproducibleai` already works with instruction modules/recipes, this function boundary depends on your existing package design.

---

## 4. Optional later: `audit_dev_governance()`
### Purpose
A richer diagnostic helper than `validate_dev_governance()`

### Possible checks
- missing files
- empty files
- suspiciously unchanged templates
- no ADRs despite design complexity
- no instructions despite package using `reproducibleai`
- no sessions folder
- maybe document-map consistency later

### Recommendation
Not needed in v1.

Start with `validate_dev_governance()`.

---

# What I do **not** recommend for v1

## `use_adr()`
As discussed, not necessary right now.

## `next_adr_number()`
Helpful only if ADR management becomes painful.

## `promote_session_updates()`
Too ambitious for now.

## file-content automation for plan/design decisions
Not needed if chat sessions are expected to draft paste-ready updates.

---

# Recommended design principles for these functions

## Principle 1: scaffold structure, not thought
Functions create the framework.  
Chats create the meaningful content.

## Principle 2: validate lightly
Do not attempt to infer whether architecture is “good.”  
Only validate whether the expected governance structure exists and is usable.

## Principle 3: align with `usethis`
These functions should feel like:
- low-friction
- additive
- transparent
- safe by default

## Principle 4: preserve composability
Do not let governance functions become entangled with:
- Shiny-specific help systems
- domain-specific templates
- repo-specific business logic

---

# Proposed default scaffold contents

## `dev/05_plan.md`
Template should include:
- title
- purpose sentence
- “how to use”
- placeholder milestone/task sections

## `dev/10_design.md`
Template should include:
- title
- purpose
- document map
- stable design overview placeholder
- open questions section

## `dev/decisions/`
Create directory with maybe:
- `.gitkeep`
or
- a short `README.md` explaining ADR purpose

I prefer a short `README.md`.

## `dev/instructions/`
Create directory with:
- `CHAT_INSTRUCTIONS.md` stub
- maybe a brief note about module composition

## `dev/sessions/`
Create directory with:
- `.gitkeep`
or small `README.md`

I slightly prefer `.gitkeep` here unless you want strong usage guidance.

## Optional `dev/40_schemas.md`
Template should include:
- purpose
- when to use
- schema sections placeholder

---

# Relationship to pkgdown

These functions should be documented in pkgdown as **repo-governance utilities**, not as user-facing package-analysis tools.

I recommend articles such as:
- “Development governance for AI-assisted repositories”
- “Using `use_dev_governance()`”
- “Document roles in `dev/`”
- “How chat sessions should promote durable decisions”

That will help users understand why the functions exist.

---

# My recommended v1 function set

## Minimal and strong
```r
use_dev_governance()
validate_dev_governance()
```

That is enough to establish the capability cleanly.

## Optional if needed soon
```r
use_instruction_entrypoint()
```

But only if the instruction framework benefits from a separate setup function.

---

# What the instruction module should rely on

The `development-governance` instruction module should assume:

- the repo may already have the scaffold
- if not, the chat can recommend `reproducibleai::use_dev_governance()`
- after scaffold exists, the chat is responsible for producing paste-ready updates to the actual docs

That is a clean division of labor.

---

# Recommended next step

I suggest we now define the **spec for `use_dev_governance()` and `validate_dev_governance()`** in more detail:

1. arguments
2. files created
3. overwrite behavior
4. output/messages
5. tests
6. pkgdown docs

That would give you a clear implementation target before we return to parameterized-help functions.

If you want, I can draft that detailed function specification next.

## @mpdougherty

I agree with this approach. Clarification question: 1. In trying to think through this refactor of the `reproducibleai` interface, when you start a new R-package repo, which comes first, defining the chat instructions, or specifying development-governance? Since development-governance is just another instruction module it seems like the existing function `use_instructions()` is still the starting point. If so, it will just need to gain some complexity to test the `spec` for modules like development-governance that are implemented via functions rather than just file copying. Am I thinking through this refactor correcly?

## Copilot

Yes — you’re thinking about the refactor correctly.

## Direct answer

**`use_instructions()` should remain the starting point.**

Because development-governance is itself an instruction module, the initial workflow for a new repo should still begin by installing the relevant instruction recipe into `dev/instructions/`.

Then, once those instructions are in place, the session or user can apply the supporting scaffold functions such as:

- `use_dev_governance()`
- later, `use_parameterized_help()`

So the order is:

1. **install the instruction framework**
2. **use that framework to drive repo setup and maintenance**
3. **apply scaffolding functions where the instructions call for them**

That is the right mental model.

---

# Why this is the right ordering

## Instructions come first conceptually
The instructions define:
- how the repo should be worked on
- what artifacts matter
- what the chat should do
- what scaffolding should be used

So they are the **governing layer**.

## Scaffolding functions come second operationally
Functions like `use_dev_governance()` are **implementation aids** that help satisfy the conventions established by the instructions.

They are not the top-level source of workflow truth.

That means:
- instructions define the contract
- scaffold functions support the contract

This is a very clean architecture.

---

# So yes: `use_instructions()` remains the entry point

That matches `reproducibleai`’s current package identity well.

Right now the package already presents itself around:
- instruction modules
- recipes
- `use_instructions()` as the mechanism for writing those instructions into a project citeturn0commentaryto=multi_tool_use.parallel0turn0commentaryto=multi_tool_use.parallel1

So the refactor should preserve that mental model.

---

# What changes, then?

The important change is that instruction modules are no longer just passive text copied into a repo.

Some modules will now have an associated **implementation spec** that says:

- this module is instructional
- but it also expects certain repo scaffolding or validation support
- and that support may come from package functions

So yes, `use_instructions()` likely needs to become more capable.

---

# Your proposed refactor direction is sound

## Current model
It sounds like the current model is roughly:
- modules/recipes exist as files
- `use_instructions()` copies them into `dev/instructions/`

## Emerging model
Now you want:
- modules to remain the primary abstraction
- but some modules to carry additional structured metadata/specification
- allowing `use_instructions()` to do more than copy files

For example, a module like `development-governance` may imply:
- copy instruction text
- maybe scaffold governance files/folders
- maybe verify repo structure
- maybe add supporting templates

That is a reasonable evolution.

---

# Recommended conceptual model

I’d frame it like this:

## Instruction modules are the primary user-facing abstraction
Users choose modules or recipes.

## Some modules are “instruction-only”
They just install guidance text.

## Some modules are “instruction + capability” modules
They install guidance text **and** may invoke supporting package behavior.

That gives you a clean extension model.

---

# Suggested refactor concept: a module spec

Yes — I think `use_instructions()` should evolve to understand some kind of module metadata/spec.

For example, each module could eventually have metadata like:

- module name
- purpose
- category
- dependencies
- whether it requires scaffolding
- which scaffold function to call
- whether it has validation support
- whether it is instruction-only or instruction-plus-capability

You don’t need to overbuild it immediately, but the conceptual shape is good.

---

# A useful distinction

I recommend distinguishing between:

## 1. instruction installation
Always handled by `use_instructions()`

## 2. capability activation
Optionally triggered by `use_instructions()` when a module spec says it has setup actions

That way you preserve backward compatibility while adding power.

---

# Practical workflow for a new repo

Here is how I think the ideal future workflow should look.

## Step 1
User runs something like:

```r
use_instructions(recipes$r_package)
```

or a recipe including:
- `chat-manual`
- `goals`
- `r-package`
- `development-governance`

## Step 2
`use_instructions()`:
- copies the instruction modules into `dev/instructions/`
- resolves dependencies
- reads module specs

## Step 3
If a module has setup actions, `use_instructions()` can either:
- **offer** to run them
- or **message** that they should be run next
- or run them automatically if explicitly requested

For example:
- `development-governance` → `use_dev_governance()`

That seems like the right UX.

---

# Recommendation on automatic behavior

I would **not** make `use_instructions()` silently run scaffold functions by default.

Better options:

## Preferred
`use_instructions()` installs the instructions and then prints actionable guidance like:

- installed module `development-governance`
- recommended next step: `reproducibleai::use_dev_governance()`

## Optional later
Allow an argument like:
- `activate = TRUE`
or
- `run_setup = TRUE`

to invoke associated setup functions.

This keeps the behavior transparent and safer.

---

# So what does this imply for the refactor?

Yes, I think it implies `use_instructions()` will need more structure.

But not necessarily a huge rewrite at first.

## Minimal viable refactor
Add a notion of module metadata that can say:
- this module has a companion setup function
- this is the recommended next step

Then `use_instructions()` can:
- copy files as before
- print smarter next-step guidance

## Later enhancement
Support actually invoking setup functions if requested.

That progression would be clean and low-risk.

---

# Recommended architecture for the refactor

## Keep
- `instructions_available()`
- `instructions_recipes()`
- `use_instructions()`

## Add
- module metadata/specs
- maybe a registry object or dataset describing modules
- scaffolding functions like `use_dev_governance()`

## Later optionally add
- setup activation from module specs
- module validation hooks

---

# My answer to your question

So yes — I think you are reasoning through this correctly:

- `use_instructions()` should remain the starting point
- development-governance is still an instruction module
- some modules will now also imply supporting package functionality
- `use_instructions()` should evolve to understand that distinction
- the first enhancement may simply be better module specs and smarter next-step guidance, not full automation

That is a strong refactor path.

---

# Strong recommendation

I would describe the new `reproducibleai` model as:

> **Instruction-first, capability-aware**

Meaning:
- workflows are still driven by instruction modules and recipes
- but modules may declare associated package capabilities
- and the package can help users activate those capabilities in a transparent way

That feels like the right design identity for `reproducibleai`.

---

# Suggested next step

The next best step is to define the **module spec / registry model** that would let `use_instructions()` support this new architecture.

Specifically:
1. what metadata a module should have
2. how `use_instructions()` should consume it
3. how setup functions are declared
4. what behavior is default vs optional

If you want, I can draft that module-spec design next.

## @mpdougherty

Ok, so if we are building `reproducibleai` on a "instruction first" model, what if we just upgrade the framework so that each instruction module is implemented by a function. This would simplify the `use_instructions()` and encapsulate the requirements for the config of each module in its own function (supporting stronger validation and reuse). If this refactor makes sense from a future-proofing perspective, it seems like now is the time to invest in this upgrade. 

## Copilot

Yes — that refactor makes sense, and I think it is a stronger long-term design than treating instruction modules as mostly static files with optional metadata.

## Direct answer

Your proposed shift to:

> **each instruction module is implemented by a function**

is a very good future-proofing move.

I think it is likely the right architecture for `reproducibleai`, especially because your package is no longer just distributing static instruction text — it is becoming a system for:

- composing reusable workflow modules
- validating module-specific requirements
- scaffolding repo structures
- evolving modules beyond simple file copy behavior

A function-based module system handles that much better.

---

# Why this is a strong design

## 1. It matches the real behavior you now want
Originally, a module may have been mostly:
- “copy this markdown file”

But now modules may need to:
- install text
- create folders/files
- validate repo state
- emit next-step guidance
- maybe branch behavior based on repo type
- maybe declare dependencies
- maybe evolve over time

That is much easier to express as a function than as a static file plus side metadata.

---

## 2. It localizes module logic
If each module has its own function, then each module can own:
- its text
- its validation rules
- its setup behavior
- its defaults
- its dependencies
- its output files

That is cleaner than having:
- one generic installer
- plus a side registry
- plus ad hoc exceptions

---

## 3. It improves validation
You already identified a major benefit:
- each module can validate its own requirements

For example:
- `development_governance_module()` can validate/create `dev/` structure
- `parameterized_help_module()` can require a `golem`-style repo context
- `r_package_module()` can check for `DESCRIPTION`

That is much more natural than trying to represent all this in static metadata.

---

## 4. It supports reuse and composition
A function-based module can:
- call shared internal helpers
- compose with other modules
- reuse templates
- make decisions based on arguments

This gives you a cleaner extensibility model.

---

## 5. It keeps the “instruction-first” philosophy intact
This is important.

You are **not** abandoning the instruction-first model.

You are just changing the implementation from:
- “modules are files”

to:
- “modules are functions that produce and install instructions, and possibly associated scaffolding”

That is still instruction-first.
It is just more powerful and better aligned with the package’s evolving purpose.

---

# Recommended mental model

I would define the new architecture as:

## Modules are executable capability units
Each module function is responsible for:
- defining its instruction content
- installing that content into the target repo
- performing any module-specific setup or checks
- returning a structured summary of what it did

That is elegant.

---

# What this means for `use_instructions()`

Under this model, `use_instructions()` becomes simpler conceptually:

## Its job becomes:
1. resolve requested modules/recipes
2. call the corresponding module functions
3. collect/report results

That is cleaner than:
- copying files
- interpreting metadata
- branching for special cases

So yes, your intuition that this could simplify `use_instructions()` is correct.

---

# Recommended architecture

## Public interface
Keep:

- `instructions_available()`
- `instructions_recipes()`
- `use_instructions()`

These are still good user-facing functions.

## Internal model
Behind the scenes, modules become functions.

Example conceptual pattern:

- `module_chat_manual()`
- `module_goals()`
- `module_r_package()`
- `module_shiny_golem()`
- `module_development_governance()`
- `module_parameterized_help()`

or similar naming.

Each module function:
- knows how to install itself
- knows what files it owns
- knows what dependencies it has
- can validate inputs and repo context

---

# What a module function should probably return

I recommend each module function return a structured object, for example containing:

- `name`
- `files_written`
- `files_skipped`
- `directories_created`
- `messages`
- `next_steps`
- `dependencies_applied`
- `warnings`

This will make `use_instructions()` much easier to summarize.

---

# Suggested responsibilities of a module function

A module function should be able to do some or all of the following:

## Required
- install the module’s instruction text into `dev/instructions/`

## Optional as needed
- scaffold required supporting files/folders
- validate repository context
- warn if prerequisites are missing
- suggest next steps
- install companion templates or examples

Not every module needs all of this, but the model supports it.

---

# Important design choice: file generation strategy

If modules are functions, you still need to decide:

## Should instruction text live:
### A. as inline strings/templates in R code
or
### B. as package inst templates that module functions write out

I strongly recommend:

## **B. store module text as package templates/resources, and let module functions install them**

Why:
- easier to edit long markdown
- easier to review in git
- avoids giant strings in R code
- cleaner separation of content and execution logic

So:
- module logic in R functions
- module content in template files in `inst/` or similar

That is probably the best architecture.

---

# Key benefit for future-proofing

This is where your proposal really shines.

If you later want modules to support:
- repo-type-specific behavior
- module arguments
- different output variants
- validation
- dependency resolution
- setup hooks
- migration behavior

a function-based module system will scale far better than static file copying.

So yes — **if you are going to make this upgrade, now is a good time**.

---

# Risks / tradeoffs

This is a good refactor, but it has some tradeoffs.

## 1. Slightly more implementation complexity now
You will need:
- a module registry
- internal conventions for module functions
- a template strategy

## 2. Need to preserve simple UX
Users should still feel like:
- “I install a recipe”

not:
- “I am orchestrating a mini plugin system”

So keep the public interface simple.

## 3. Need strong conventions
To avoid module drift, define:
- naming conventions
- return object structure
- where templates live
- what module functions are allowed to do

This is manageable, but important.

---

# My recommendation

## Yes — do this refactor.
I think it is the right design move.

### Specifically:
- keep the public API instruction-centric
- implement each module as a function
- store module text as templates/resources
- let module functions own their own validation/setup logic
- keep `use_instructions()` as the high-level orchestrator

That gives you:
- future-proofing
- modularity
- stronger validation
- cleaner extensibility
- easier support for governance and parameterized-help capabilities

---

# Recommended design principle

I’d describe the new model as:

> **Instructions are installed by executable modules, not passive files.**

That is a strong identity for `reproducibleai`.

---

# Suggested next step

The next best step is to define the **module-function architecture spec**, including:

1. module naming convention
2. template storage convention
3. function inputs
4. function return structure
5. how recipes resolve modules
6. what `use_instructions()` does under the new model
7. what behavior is allowed inside a module

If you want, I can draft that architecture spec next.

## @mpdougherty

I want to emphasize that what I like most about the "modular chat instructions" architecture that I've implemented so far: 1. natural language static text definition of instructions. 2. instructions easily reviewable by non programmers. 3. simple UX: "compose a recipe". 4. static instructions stored in `inst/instructions` make version updates clear and obvious. 5. destination package instructions are consistent across team repos and not needlessly complexified with customizations across implementations. 6. minimizes complexity to help reviewers better comprehend what we are deploying in a really new domain where little experience and comfort exists. 7. module functions don't modify static instruction text, just handle the behavior needed to config them for use in the target repo. Conclusion: Does this compromise position make sense?

## Copilot

Yes — that compromise makes excellent sense, and I think it is the best design.

## Direct answer

You should **keep instruction modules as static natural-language artifacts** and use **module functions only as installation/configuration handlers**.

That preserves the strengths you value most:

- reviewable static instruction text
- simple “compose a recipe” UX
- visible versioned instruction content in `inst/instructions`
- low conceptual complexity
- strong consistency across repos
- minimal hidden behavior

while still giving you:
- module-specific setup/config support
- stronger validation
- a path for future extensibility

So yes — I strongly agree with this compromise architecture.

---

# Why this is the right balance

Your list of priorities is exactly the reason **not** to move to a fully code-defined instruction system.

If you made modules primarily code objects, you would lose some of the best properties of your current approach:

## You would weaken:
- human reviewability
- transparency of what instructions actually say
- ease of diffing changes in Git
- trust from collaborators who are not programmers
- the clarity of “the module is this markdown file”

And those are not minor benefits — they are central benefits.

---

# The right separation of concerns

The architecture that best fits your goals is:

## Static instruction text = the authoritative instruction content
Stored in:
- `inst/instructions/...`

These remain:
- plain language
- easy to diff
- easy to review
- easy to discuss
- stable and explicit

## Module functions = operational support
Used only to:
- install/copy/render the module into the target repo
- handle module-specific setup
- validate prerequisites
- scaffold companion files when appropriate
- provide messages/next steps

But **not** to rewrite or dynamically generate the instruction text itself.

That is the key boundary.

---

# Your compromise model, stated clearly

I would formulate it like this:

> **Instruction modules remain static, versioned natural-language artifacts. Module functions exist only to support installation, validation, and repo-specific configuration required to use those instructions correctly.**

That is a very strong design principle.

---

# Why this is future-proof enough

This compromise still gives you the extensibility benefits you wanted.

Because module functions can still:
- create `dev/` scaffolding
- add required CSS
- validate repo structure
- install supporting files
- warn about missing prerequisites
- manage module-specific config behavior

without turning the instruction content itself into opaque program logic.

So you get:
- extensibility where it matters
- stability/transparency where it matters most

That’s excellent architecture.

---

# Recommended model for `reproducibleai`

## 1. Keep modules as static instruction files
These remain the canonical instruction payloads.

## 2. Add optional module handlers
For modules that need setup/config support, define companion functions.

For example conceptually:
- static instruction file: `development-governance.md`
- optional handler: `handle_module_development_governance()`

And similarly for:
- `parameterized-help`
- maybe `shiny-golem` later if needed

## 3. Keep recipes as simple compositions of static modules
This preserves your current UX:
- choose a recipe
- install a set of modules

## 4. Let `use_instructions()` orchestrate both
It should:
- install the static instruction files
- optionally invoke module handlers where defined
- report what happened clearly

That gives you the upgrade path without sacrificing the original philosophy.

---

# Why this is better than “everything is a function”

A fully function-based module system is elegant technically, but it conflicts with several of your strongest values:

- transparent review
- static version clarity
- low perceived complexity
- easy trust-building in a new domain

Your compromise preserves those values.

Given your team context, that matters more than maximal technical abstraction.

---

# Suggested design rule

I recommend adopting this explicit rule in `reproducibleai`:

## Rule
**Module handlers may configure the target repo, but they must not alter the canonical wording of the static instruction module text.**

That is a very important constraint.

You can allow handlers to:
- install companion files
- populate templates
- scaffold structure
- validate readiness

But the instruction text copied from `inst/instructions` should remain the same reviewed artifact.

---

# What this implies for `use_instructions()`

`use_instructions()` can evolve modestly rather than radically.

## It should:
1. resolve the requested modules/recipe
2. copy the static instruction files into the target repo
3. check whether any installed modules have companion handlers
4. run those handlers if requested, or at least message clearly about recommended next steps
5. summarize what was installed and configured

That is all very manageable.

---

# Recommended handler behavior

I would also recommend that companion handlers be:

- **optional**
- **transparent**
- **idempotent**
- **narrowly scoped**
- **non-destructive by default**

That will help preserve trust.

---

# Best conceptual model

I think the package should now be described as:

> **Static instruction-first, function-assisted configuration**

That’s a much better description than either:
- file-only
or
- everything-as-code

It captures the compromise exactly.

---

# My recommendation

Yes — adopt this compromise.

It is:
- consistent with your current strengths
- easier for your team to understand
- easier to review
- safer in a new workflow domain
- still extensible enough for governance and parameterized-help

I think this is the right architecture.

---

# Suggested next step

Now that the architecture is settled, the next best step is to define the **module-handler spec**:

1. how static modules declare whether they have a handler
2. how `use_instructions()` discovers and invokes handlers
3. what handlers are allowed to do
4. what their inputs/outputs look like
5. how this applies first to `development-governance`

If you want, I can draft that module-handler architecture next.

## Direct answer

- named predictably, e.g. `module_<module_name>()`

---

# Why this is a strong design

This gives you the best of both worlds:

## You keep
- static instruction text as the authoritative reviewed artifact
- simple recipe composition UX
- transparent versioned module content
- consistent repo outputs

## And you gain
- a uniform execution model
- simpler `use_instructions()`
- no need for special-case metadata discovery
- future room for module-specific config logic

That’s elegant.

---

# Why the “always have a handler” rule is useful

I think this is better than having handlers only for some modules.

Because then `use_instructions()` can follow one simple pattern:

- resolve modules
- for each module, call `module_<name>()`

No branching like:
- “is this one file-only?”
- “does this one have metadata?”
- “does this one have a companion function?”

That consistency matters.

---

# Recommended architecture

## Static instruction files remain in package resources
For example in:
- `inst/instructions/modules/<module>.md`
or your existing equivalent structure

These are still the canonical text artifacts.

## Every module gets a handler
Example conceptual names:
- `module_chat_manual()`
- `module_goals()`
- `module_r_package()`
- `module_shiny_golem()`
- `module_development_governance()`
- `module_parameterized_help()`

## Every handler does two things
### Part A — install static instruction text
Copy/write the module markdown into:
- `dev/instructions/`

### Part B — perform config/setup
Examples:
- nothing
- scaffold directories/files
- verify repo context
- write companion files
- issue post-install guidance

If unused, this section stays empty.

That is exactly the right structure.

---

# Recommended design principle

I would phrase the rule as:

> Every instruction module is represented by a static instruction file and a corresponding handler function. The static file is the canonical instruction content. The handler installs that content and performs any module-specific repository configuration required to support its use.

That should probably become part of `reproducibleai`’s own design documentation.

---

# Suggested `use_instructions()` behavior under this model

This becomes very straightforward.

## `use_instructions()` should:
1. resolve requested modules from the user input or recipe
2. normalize module names
3. call `module_<module_name>()` for each module in order
4. collect results
5. print a concise summary

That is much simpler than a plugin-discovery or metadata-heavy system.

---

# Suggested handler function contract

I recommend each handler take a consistent minimal argument set, something like conceptually:

- target repo path
- overwrite behavior
- maybe verbosity
- maybe config list later if needed

Even if you don’t expose much yet, consistency will help.

Example conceptual signature:
```r
module_development_governance(path = ".", overwrite = FALSE, ...)
```

That keeps the design extensible.

---

# Suggested handler responsibilities

## Required in every handler
- locate the static instruction file for that module
- copy/write it into `dev/instructions/`

## Optional in every handler
- create supporting files/folders
- validate assumptions
- emit messages/warnings
- suggest next steps

## Not allowed
- silently alter the canonical module text
- inject repo-specific wording into the installed instruction content
- perform surprising destructive actions by default

That keeps behavior transparent.

---

# Good consequence for future modules

This convention means future modules can start simple:

## v1
- static text copied
- config section empty

## later
- same handler gains setup logic

No interface redesign needed.

That is exactly the kind of future-proofing you want.

---

# Recommendation for file naming consistency

If module names are hyphenated in user-facing form, I recommend deciding one normalization rule for function names.

For example:

- module file / public module name: `development-governance`
- handler function: `module_development_governance()`

That is intuitive and conventional in R.

So:
- public names can stay kebab-case
- function names should use underscores

That will make both the UX and codebase clean.

---

# Recommended return value

Each handler should probably return a structured result, even if simple.

For example:
- module name
- instruction file installed
- any directories/files created
- any warnings
- any next steps

This will make `use_instructions()` summaries much easier.

But this can stay lightweight.

---

# My assessment

This is a better design than:
- file-only modules
- or fully code-defined modules
- or optional-handler discovery

It gives you:
- transparency
- predictability
- simplicity
- extensibility

So yes, I agree with your refinement.

---

Now that this is settled, the next best step is to define the **module handler spec** concretely:

1. naming rules
2. source file locations
3. target file locations
4. handler arguments
5. handler return structure
6. `use_instructions()` orchestration behavior
7. first implementation target: `module_development_governance()`

## @mpdougherty

Agreed. Proceed. 

## Copilot

Great — here is the **module handler architecture spec** I recommend for `MVR-GIS/reproducibleai`.

This is designed to preserve your static-instruction-first philosophy while standardizing how modules are installed and configured.

---

# Module handler architecture spec

## 1. Core design principle

Each instruction module must have:

1. a **static instruction file** stored in the package
2. a corresponding **handler function** named:

```r
module_<module_name>()
```

where:
- the public module name remains kebab-case, e.g. `development-governance`
- the handler function name uses snake_case, e.g. `module_development_governance()`

## Canonical rule
- the static instruction file is the **authoritative instruction text**
- the handler function is responsible for:
  1. copying/installing that text into the target repository
  2. performing any module-specific configuration required for use in that repository

If no configuration is currently needed, the configuration step is still present conceptually but performs no action.

---

# 2. Goals of this architecture

This architecture is intended to preserve:

- natural-language static instruction definitions
- easy review by non-programmers
- simple “compose a recipe” UX
- visible versioned instruction text in package source
- consistent deployed instructions across repos
- low complexity and high transparency

while also enabling:

- module-specific setup/configuration
- stronger validation
- future extensibility without redesigning the public interface

---

# 3. Static instruction source convention

## Recommended location
Store static module text under a package directory such as:

```text
inst/instructions/modules/
```

with one file per module, for example:

```text
inst/instructions/modules/chat-manual.md
inst/instructions/modules/goals.md
inst/instructions/modules/r-package.md
inst/instructions/modules/shiny-golem.md
inst/instructions/modules/development-governance.md
inst/instructions/modules/parameterized-help.md
```

## Canonical text rule
These files are the reviewed, version-controlled, canonical instruction contents.

Handler functions must not dynamically rewrite or customize the substantive wording of these module texts.

---

# 4. Target installation convention

Installed module text should be written into the target repository under:

```text
dev/instructions/
```

using predictable file names derived from the module name, e.g.:

```text
dev/instructions/development-governance.md
```

If your package already uses a different filename convention for deployed instructions, keep that convention consistent. The important rule is predictability and consistency.

---

# 5. Handler naming convention

For every module name in kebab-case:

```text
<module-name>
```

define a handler function:

```r
module_<module_name>()
```

where hyphens become underscores.

## Examples

| Public module name | Handler function |
|---|---|
| `chat-manual` | `module_chat_manual()` |
| `r-package` | `module_r_package()` |
| `shiny-golem` | `module_shiny_golem()` |
| `development-governance` | `module_development_governance()` |
| `parameterized-help` | `module_parameterized_help()` |

This mapping should be deterministic and internalized by `use_instructions()`.

---

# 6. Required handler responsibilities

Every handler function must perform these two responsibilities.

## Part A — install the static instruction text
The handler must:
- locate the canonical static instruction file in the package
- copy/write it into the target repo’s `dev/instructions/`
- respect overwrite behavior

## Part B — perform module-specific configuration
The handler may:
- create supporting files/folders
- scaffold required repo artifacts
- validate repo context
- provide messages or next steps
- create companion templates where appropriate

If no configuration is required yet, this part performs no action.

---

# 7. Handler behavior constraints

Handlers are allowed to:
- create missing directories
- install supporting files/templates
- validate or warn about missing repo structure
- message about recommended next steps

Handlers must not:
- silently alter the substantive instruction text
- inject repo-specific editorial customizations into the module markdown
- perform destructive actions by default
- overwrite existing files unless explicitly allowed
- create hidden behavior that reviewers cannot easily understand

This keeps deployment transparent.

---

# 8. Recommended handler signature

I recommend each handler use a consistent interface, conceptually like:

```r
module_<name>(
  path = ".",
  overwrite = FALSE,
  ...
)
```

## Recommended arguments

### `path`
Target repository root.

### `overwrite`
Whether existing installed instruction files or scaffold files may be overwritten.

Default should be conservative.

### `...`
Reserved for future extensibility.

You may later add arguments such as:
- `quiet = FALSE`
- `configure = TRUE`
- `check = TRUE`

but I recommend keeping v1 minimal.

---

# 9. Recommended handler return value

Each handler should return a structured result object, even if lightweight.

Recommended fields:

- `module_name`
- `instruction_source`
- `instruction_target`
- `files_written`
- `files_skipped`
- `dirs_created`
- `warnings`
- `next_steps`

This lets `use_instructions()` summarize actions clearly.

A simple named list is sufficient for v1.

---

# 10. Recommended internal helper pattern

To avoid duplication, you should probably create small internal helpers such as:

- internal helper to locate a module’s canonical instruction file
- internal helper to install a module markdown file into `dev/instructions/`
- internal helper to normalize module name ↔ handler name mapping
- internal helper to accumulate result objects

This keeps module handlers concise.

For example, many handlers will mostly differ only in:
- source module name
- config actions performed after installation

---

# 11. `use_instructions()` orchestration model

Under this architecture, `use_instructions()` should:

1. resolve the requested modules or recipe into an ordered vector of module names
2. normalize module names
3. map each module name to its handler function
4. call each handler in order
5. collect results
6. print a concise summary of:
   - modules installed
   - files written/skipped
   - additional configuration performed
   - recommended next steps

This keeps `use_instructions()` simple and generic.

---

# 12. Recipe model

Recipes should remain simple compositions of public module names.

Example conceptual structure:

```r
c("chat-manual", "goals", "r-package", "development-governance")
```

`use_instructions()` resolves the recipe, then delegates installation/configuration to each module handler.

So recipes remain:
- simple
- readable
- reviewable

That preserves one of your most valuable UX properties.

---

# 13. Relationship between static text and config behavior

This is a critical rule.

## Static text
Defines:
- what the module instructs
- what reviewers read
- what is versioned visibly in Git

## Handler behavior
Defines:
- how the module is installed
- what repo setup is needed to support it
- any validation or scaffolding that operationalizes the module

### Non-negotiable constraint
Handler behavior must support the instruction module, not redefine it.

That is the heart of the compromise architecture.

---

# 14. Module categories

This architecture supports both kinds of modules cleanly.

## Instruction-only in practice
Examples:
- `chat-manual`
- `goals`

These still have handlers, but config behavior is effectively empty.

## Instruction + config
Examples:
- `development-governance`
- `parameterized-help`
- possibly `shiny-golem` later

These handlers install the instruction text and also scaffold or validate repo structure.

This gives you one uniform execution model.

---

# 15. Initial implementation priority

I recommend implementing this architecture in the following order:

## Phase 1
Refactor existing modules so each has a handler that at least installs the static text.

## Phase 2
Update `use_instructions()` to call handlers rather than copy files directly.

## Phase 3
Implement meaningful config behavior for:
- `module_development_governance()`

## Phase 4
Implement meaningful config behavior for:
- `module_parameterized_help()`

That sequence reduces risk.

---

# 16. Recommended v1 behavior for `module_development_governance()`

This should be your first “instruction + config” handler.

## It should:
### A. install:
- `dev/instructions/development-governance.md`

### B. scaffold:
- `dev/05_plan.md`
- `dev/10_design.md`
- `dev/decisions/`
- `dev/instructions/` if missing
- `dev/sessions/`

Optional:
- `dev/40_schemas.md`

### C. respect:
- no overwrite by default

### D. report:
- what was created
- what already existed
- recommended next steps

This makes it the first proof of the handler architecture.

---

# 17. Recommended tests

This architecture should be tested at two levels.

## A. Generic handler/orchestration tests
- module name maps correctly to handler name
- handler installs correct instruction file
- overwrite behavior works
- `use_instructions()` calls handlers in recipe order

## B. Module-specific tests
For `module_development_governance()`:
- required files/directories are created
- existing files are preserved when `overwrite = FALSE`
- result object reports correct actions

These tests will be important because the package is moving from simple file copying to behavior-backed modules.

---

# 18. Recommended pkgdown documentation

Document this model clearly in pkgdown.

At minimum:
- explain that instruction modules are still static reviewed text
- explain that each module has a handler function
- explain that handlers may perform setup/configuration
- explain that recipes remain simple module compositions
- explain which modules currently have meaningful config behavior

This transparency will help users trust the system.

---

# 19. Recommended design summary

I would summarize the architecture like this:

> Each `reproducibleai` instruction module consists of a canonical static markdown instruction file plus a corresponding handler function. The handler installs the static text into the target repository and performs any module-specific configuration needed to support that module. This preserves reviewable, versioned instruction content while enabling transparent, extensible repo setup behavior.

That is the core design statement.

---

# Recommendation

This spec is now strong enough to support implementation planning.

## Best next step
I recommend we now draft the **function specification for `module_development_governance()`** first, including:
- files/directories created
- template contents
- overwrite behavior
- result object structure
- tests
- pkgdown docs

That would give you the first concrete implementation target under the new architecture.

If you want, I can draft that next.

- adding another directory layer would not add meaningful clarity

- handler functions map directly to those files there

That keeps the package simpler.

# Updated convention

## Canonical source location
Static instruction files live in:

```text
inst/instructions/
```

For example:

```text
inst/instructions/chat-manual.md
inst/instructions/goals.md
inst/instructions/r-package.md
inst/instructions/shiny-golem.md
inst/instructions/development-governance.md
inst/instructions/parameterized-help.md
```

## Canonical rule
These files remain:
- the authoritative natural-language instruction content
- version-controlled and reviewer-friendly
- unmodified by handler functions except for installation into the target repo

That is fully consistent with your design principles.

---

# Why this is the right choice

This preserves:
- fewer moving parts
- clearer file discovery
- less path complexity in handler logic
- continuity with your existing implementation

And it avoids creating an unnecessary abstraction layer.

So yes — I think this clarification improves the design.

---

# Updated module handler spec note

The relevant portion of the spec should now read conceptually as:

> Static instruction source files are stored directly in `inst/instructions/`. Each handler function installs its corresponding file from that location into the target repository’s `dev/instructions/` directory, then performs any module-specific configuration required for use.

That is likely the final version of the source-location rule.

---

# Next step: `module_development_governance()` function spec

Below is the detailed function specification for the first concrete handler.

---

# Function spec: `module_development_governance()`

## Purpose
Install the `development-governance` instruction module into a target repository and scaffold the standard internal `dev/` governance structure required to support that module.

This is the first example of an instruction module whose handler performs meaningful repo configuration beyond copying static instruction text.

---

## Responsibilities

### Part A — install instruction text
Copy:

```text
inst/instructions/development-governance.md
```

to:

```text
dev/instructions/development-governance.md
```

in the target repository.

### Part B — scaffold governance structure
Ensure the target repository contains the expected governance artifacts:

Required:
- `dev/`
- `dev/05_plan.md`
- `dev/10_design.md`
- `dev/decisions/`
- `dev/instructions/`
- `dev/sessions/`

Optional for now:
- `dev/40_schemas.md`

I recommend **not** creating `dev/40_schemas.md` by default in v1 unless you want it universally present. My preference is to leave it optional and add later only when needed.

---

## Recommended function signature

```r
module_development_governance(
  path = ".",
  overwrite = FALSE,
  ...
)
```

### Arguments

#### `path`
Path to the target repository root.

Default:
```r
"."
```

#### `overwrite`
Whether existing scaffold files or installed instruction files may be overwritten.

Default:
```r
FALSE
```

Meaning:
- existing files are preserved by default
- missing files are created
- existing directories are left alone

#### `...`
Reserved for future extensibility.

---

## File and directory creation behavior

## Directories to ensure exist
- `dev/`
- `dev/decisions/`
- `dev/instructions/`
- `dev/sessions/`

## Files to create if missing
- `dev/05_plan.md`
- `dev/10_design.md`
- `dev/instructions/development-governance.md`

## Optional companion files
I recommend:

### For `dev/decisions/`
Create a small README to explain directory purpose:

```text
dev/decisions/README.md
```

This is useful because ADR folders otherwise look empty and ambiguous.

### For `dev/sessions/`
I recommend **no README** initially.
An empty directory marker may be enough, or simply create the directory and let git track it once a session file exists.

If you need a tracked artifact, a short README is acceptable, but I’d keep it minimal.

---

# Template content recommendations

## `dev/05_plan.md`
This should be a lightweight template, not a long manual.

Recommended sections:
- title
- short purpose statement
- how to use
- “Now” section
- milestone/task placeholders

### Suggested template shape
```markdown
# Project Plan

Last updated: YYYY-MM-DD

## Purpose
This file is the canonical ordered task list for active development work.

## How to use
- Keep tasks small and concrete.
- Record definitions of done where helpful.
- Update this file when design discussions create follow-up work.
- When resuming work, read this file and `dev/10_design.md`.

## Now
- [ ] Add immediate next task

## Upcoming
- [ ] Add next milestone or task group
```

## `dev/10_design.md`
This should establish the document’s role clearly.

Recommended sections:
- title
- purpose
- document map
- current design overview
- open questions

### Suggested template shape
```markdown
# Design

Last updated: YYYY-MM-DD

## Purpose
This document records the current stable architecture, operating assumptions, and capability boundaries for the repository.

## Document map
- Plan: `dev/05_plan.md`
- Decisions: `dev/decisions/`
- Instructions: `dev/instructions/`
- Sessions: `dev/sessions/`

## Current design
Add stable architecture and design notes here.

## Open questions
- [ ] Add unresolved design questions here
```

## `dev/decisions/README.md`
Recommended short explanation:

```markdown
# Decision Records

This directory stores durable design and architecture decisions.

Use this directory for decisions that:
- materially affect future work
- involve tradeoffs or alternatives
- should remain easy to review over time

Do not use this directory for task tracking or transient session notes.
```

---

# Overwrite behavior

## Default
`overwrite = FALSE`

### Behavior
- create missing files/directories
- preserve existing files
- do not replace user-edited content
- still install any missing instruction file

## When `overwrite = TRUE`
- replace scaffolded files owned by this handler
- replace installed module file
- preserve directories
- avoid touching unrelated files

This behavior should be explicit and predictable.

---

# Result object

I recommend returning a named list like:

```r
list(
  module_name = "development-governance",
  instruction_source = "<pkg path>",
  instruction_target = "<repo path>/dev/instructions/development-governance.md",
  dirs_created = c(...),
  files_written = c(...),
  files_skipped = c(...),
  warnings = c(...),
  next_steps = c(...)
)
```

## Recommended `next_steps`
Examples:
- review `dev/05_plan.md`
- review `dev/10_design.md`
- begin promoting durable design decisions into `dev/decisions/`
- use the installed `development-governance` instructions in future chat sessions

This makes the handler output genuinely useful.

---

# Messaging behavior

I recommend concise, `usethis`-like messages such as:
- created `dev/`
- wrote `dev/05_plan.md`
- left existing `dev/10_design.md` unchanged
- installed `dev/instructions/development-governance.md`

And then a short summary.

This should be informative but not noisy.

---

# Error handling

## Should error if:
- `path` does not exist
- `path` is not a directory
- package source instruction file cannot be found

## Should warn, not error, if:
- expected repo markers are missing
  - e.g. no `.Rproj`, no `DESCRIPTION`
  
Because governance scaffolding could be useful in more than one repo type.

That said, if you want this function scoped to package-like repos only, that can be tightened later.

---

# Tests

## Core tests
### installs instruction file
- creates `dev/instructions/development-governance.md`

### scaffolds required files/directories
- creates `dev/`
- creates `dev/05_plan.md`
- creates `dev/10_design.md`
- creates `dev/decisions/`
- creates `dev/instructions/`
- creates `dev/sessions/`

### preserves existing files when `overwrite = FALSE`
- existing plan/design files remain unchanged

### replaces scaffolded files when `overwrite = TRUE`
- existing scaffold-owned files are updated

### returns structured result
- result contains expected fields

## Nice-to-have tests
- `dev/decisions/README.md` created
- messages are emitted as expected

---

# pkgdown/reference documentation

Document `module_development_governance()` as:

## Purpose
Install the `development-governance` instruction module and scaffold the internal `dev/` governance framework for an AI-assisted repository.

## Emphasize
- this function supports the instruction module
- it does not write substantive plan/design/decision content for the user
- chat sessions are still expected to provide paste-ready updates during real development work

That distinction matters.

---

# My recommendation on optional schema doc

You raised an important design choice implicitly.

## Recommendation
Do **not** create `dev/40_schemas.md` by default in `module_development_governance()` v1.

Why:
- not every repo needs it
- it adds noise if unused
- it’s better introduced when structural contracts actually emerge

If later you want it, you can:
- add an argument
- or let another module scaffold it

---

# Strong recommendation

This is a solid first implementation target because it proves all the important parts of the new architecture:

- static instruction file remains canonical
- handler performs installation
- handler performs repo config
- no needlessly dynamic instruction text
- setup supports, but does not replace, instruction-driven chat workflow

---

I recommend next defining either:

1. **the refactor spec for `use_instructions()`** under the handler architecture, or  
2. **the validation companion function**, likely `validate_dev_governance()`

I recommend **`use_instructions()` next**, because the handler architecture depends on it.
