# Now proceeding with Option 1:
# `use_instructions()` refactor spec under the module-handler architecture

Below is the recommended refactor specification.

---

# Function spec: `use_instructions()`

## Purpose
Install one or more instruction modules into a target repository by resolving module names and recipes, then invoking the handler function for each module in order.

Under the refactored architecture, `use_instructions()` is the high-level orchestration function for the instruction-first workflow.

It remains the primary public entry point for adopting `reproducibleai` conventions in a repository.

---

# Core design role

`use_instructions()` should:

1. accept module names or recipes
2. resolve them to an ordered set of public module names
3. map each public module name to its corresponding handler function
4. call each handler in sequence
5. collect and summarize results

It should **not**:
- directly implement module-specific setup logic
- directly copy module files itself except through handlers
- hard-code special-case behavior for specific modules where handlers already encapsulate that behavior

That logic belongs in module handlers.

---

# Public API role

This function remains the main user-facing workflow entry point.

That means the UX still looks like:

```r
use_instructions(c("chat-manual", "goals", "development-governance"))
```

or:

```r
use_instructions(recipes$r_package)
```

This preserves the simple “compose a recipe” model.

---

# Recommended function signature

Conceptually:

```r
use_instructions(
  modules,
  path = ".",
  overwrite = FALSE,
  ...
)
```

## Arguments

### `modules`
A character vector of public module names, or a recipe-expanded vector of module names.

Examples:
```r
c("chat-manual", "goals", "development-governance")
```

### `path`
Path to the target repository root.

Default:
```r
"."
```

### `overwrite`
Passed through to module handlers.

Default:
```r
FALSE
```

### `...`
Reserved for future extensibility.

Potential later use:
- `quiet = FALSE`
- `stop_on_error = TRUE`
- `deduplicate = TRUE`

But v1 should stay simple.

---

# Required orchestration behavior

## 1. Normalize input
`use_instructions()` should accept:
- a character vector of module names
- output from `instructions_recipes()`

It should normalize into an ordered character vector of public module names.

## 2. Deduplicate modules while preserving order
If the same module appears multiple times through recipe composition, it should only be run once.

Recommended rule:
- keep first occurrence
- drop subsequent duplicates

This avoids repeated installation/configuration.

## 3. Validate module names
Before running handlers:
- confirm each module name is known
- if unknown, error clearly and list available modules

## 4. Resolve handler function names
Map:
- `development-governance`
to:
- `module_development_governance`

by replacing hyphens with underscores and prepending `module_`.

## 5. Invoke handlers in order
Call each handler in sequence with:
- `path`
- `overwrite`
- any future shared args

## 6. Collect results
Store each handler’s result object in order.

## 7. Print summary
At the end, print a concise summary of:
- modules processed
- files written/skipped
- directories created
- warnings
- next steps

---

# Handler resolution rules

## Public-to-handler mapping
Use deterministic mapping:
```text
<module-name> -> module_<module_name>()
```

where:
- hyphens become underscores

Examples:
- `chat-manual` → `module_chat_manual`
- `r-package` → `module_r_package`
- `development-governance` → `module_development_governance`

## Resolution failure
If a public module name exists but the corresponding handler function does not:
- error clearly
- indicate that the module is incompletely implemented in the package

This is preferable to silent fallback behavior.

---

# Recommended return value

`use_instructions()` should return a structured summary object, likely a named list, containing:

- `modules_requested`
- `modules_processed`
- `results` (list of per-module handler results)
- `files_written`
- `files_skipped`
- `dirs_created`
- `warnings`
- `next_steps`

This gives both:
- good programmatic usability
- a clear printed summary

Flattening aggregate file/dir fields from the handler results will be helpful.

---

# Messaging behavior

The user experience should feel:
- clear
- lightweight
- transparent

## Recommended messaging pattern
### At start
```text
Installing instruction modules into <path>
```

### Per module
```text
[1/3] Installing module: development-governance
```

### At end
```text
Done. Installed 3 instruction modules.
Created 4 directories and 6 files.
Next steps:
- review dev/10_design.md
- review dev/40_schemas.md
```

That is enough without being noisy.

---

# Error handling

## Should error when:
- `modules` is empty
- unknown module names are supplied
- `path` does not exist
- `path` is not a directory
- a required handler function is missing
- a handler errors and you choose default `stop_on_error = TRUE`

## Future option
Later you may want:
- `stop_on_error = FALSE`

But I recommend defaulting to fail-fast in v1.

That will be easier to reason about.

---

# Relationship to recipes

`instructions_recipes()` should continue to return simple vectors of public module names.

No change is needed to the recipe concept itself.

This is important:
- recipes remain static and reviewable
- `use_instructions()` remains the only orchestrator
- handler complexity stays hidden behind a simple user interface

That is exactly the right balance.

---

# Relationship to static instruction files

`use_instructions()` should never directly manage instruction file contents.

Instead:
- each handler owns installation of its own canonical static text from `inst/instructions/`

This keeps the architecture clean:
- `use_instructions()` orchestrates
- handlers implement
- static files define content

---

# Suggested internal helper functions

To keep `use_instructions()` simple, I recommend internal helpers such as:

## `normalize_module_names()`
- validates input shape
- deduplicates while preserving order

## `module_name_to_handler()`
- converts public module names to handler function names

## `get_module_handler()`
- resolves handler function object
- errors clearly if missing

## `summarize_module_results()`
- flattens per-handler result objects into aggregate summary

These helpers are likely worth having.

---

# Example workflow

## Example 1 — simple module set
```r
use_instructions(
  c("chat-manual", "goals", "development-governance"),
  path = "."
)
```

Behavior:
- call `module_chat_manual()`
- call `module_goals()`
- call `module_development_governance()`

## Example 2 — recipe-based
```r
recipes <- instructions_recipes()
use_instructions(recipes$r_package, path = ".")
```

Behavior:
- resolve modules from recipe
- deduplicate if needed
- call handlers in order

---

# Tests for `use_instructions()`

## Core tests

### resolves and runs handlers in order
Mock handlers or use temporary fixtures to verify order.

### deduplicates repeated modules
If input repeats a module, it runs once.

### errors on unknown module names
Error message should list valid module names.

### errors if handler missing
A known module without a handler should fail clearly.

### passes `path` and `overwrite` through
Verify handlers receive shared arguments.

### aggregates results correctly
Returned summary object contains expected combined fields.

---

# Interaction with `module_development_governance()`

Because of your schema clarification, once this handler exists it should scaffold:

- `dev/05_plan.md`
- `dev/10_design.md`
- `dev/40_schemas.md`
- `dev/decisions/`
- `dev/instructions/`
- `dev/sessions/`

So the development-governance handler becomes the first strong test case for the orchestrator.

---

# Updated recommendation for governance scaffold

The required governance scaffold is now:

- `dev/05_plan.md`
- `dev/10_design.md`
- `dev/40_schemas.md`
- `dev/decisions/`
- `dev/instructions/`
- `dev/sessions/`

This should now be reflected consistently everywhere going forward.

---

# Strong recommendation

This refactor keeps everything you value:

- static, reviewable module text
- simple recipe UX
- visible versioning
- minimal conceptual complexity

while making the system more robust and extensible through:
- mandatory handlers
- centralized orchestration
- module-local config behavior

That is a strong architecture.

---

## Best next step
I recommend next defining the **updated detailed spec for `module_development_governance()`**, revised to make `dev/40_schemas.md` required, and then after that:
- tests for the handler
- and then parameterized-help handler design

If you want, I can draft that revised `module_development_governance()` spec next in final form.

## @mpdougherty

I concur. Proceed. 

## Copilot

Great — here is the **revised final-form specification for `module_development_governance()`**, updated so that `dev/40_schemas.md` is a required part of the scaffold.

---

# Function spec: `module_development_governance()`

## Purpose
Install the `development-governance` instruction module into a target repository and scaffold the standard `dev/` governance structure used to maintain durable development-state documentation in AI-assisted, package-centered data science projects.

This handler is the first core example of the new architecture in which:

- the module’s canonical instruction text remains a static markdown file in `inst/instructions/`
- the module handler installs that static text into the target repository
- the module handler performs the repo configuration needed to support that module’s intended use

---

# Core responsibilities

`module_development_governance()` has two responsibilities.

## Part A — install instruction text
Install the canonical static instruction file:

```text
inst/instructions/development-governance.md
```

into the target repository at:

```text
dev/instructions/development-governance.md
```

This file remains the reviewed, authoritative instruction text.

## Part B — scaffold the development-governance framework
Ensure the target repository contains the required governance artifacts:

### Required directories
- `dev/`
- `dev/decisions/`
- `dev/instructions/`
- `dev/sessions/`

### Required files
- `dev/05_plan.md`
- `dev/10_design.md`
- `dev/40_schemas.md`
- `dev/decisions/README.md`
- `dev/instructions/development-governance.md`

This establishes the minimum durable governance structure expected by the `development-governance` instruction module.

---

# Design rationale

This scaffold reflects the view that AI-assisted data science repos need explicit durable artifacts for:

- active work planning
- stable architecture and design
- structural/data contracts
- design decisions
- reusable instructions
- session history

In particular, `dev/40_schemas.md` is required because undocumented or implicit schemas are a common source of fragile and buggy data-science workflows.

---

# Recommended function signature

```r
module_development_governance(
  path = ".",
  overwrite = FALSE,
  ...
)
```

## Arguments

### `path`
Path to the target repository root.

Default:
```r
"."
```

### `overwrite`
Logical. Whether existing governance files owned by this handler may be overwritten.

Default:
```r
FALSE
```

With `overwrite = FALSE`:
- create missing files and directories
- preserve existing files
- do not replace user-edited content

With `overwrite = TRUE`:
- replace files that this handler owns
- preserve existing directories
- avoid touching unrelated files

### `...`
Reserved for future extensibility.

No additional arguments are required in v1.

---

# Required file creation behavior

## Directories to ensure exist
The handler must ensure the existence of:

- `dev/`
- `dev/decisions/`
- `dev/instructions/`
- `dev/sessions/`

If a directory already exists, leave it unchanged.

## Files to create if missing
The handler must create the following files if they do not exist, or overwrite them only when `overwrite = TRUE`:

- `dev/05_plan.md`
- `dev/10_design.md`
- `dev/40_schemas.md`
- `dev/decisions/README.md`
- `dev/instructions/development-governance.md`

---

# Template content recommendations

The scaffolded files should be concise, useful, and easy for both humans and chat sessions to extend.

## `dev/05_plan.md`
### Purpose
Serve as the canonical active work plan.

### Recommended template
````markdown
# Project Plan

Last updated: YYYY-MM-DD

## Purpose
This file is the canonical ordered task list for active development work.

## How to use
- Keep tasks small and concrete.
- Record definitions of done where helpful.
- Update this file when design discussions create follow-up work.
- When resuming work, read this file and `dev/10_design.md`.

## Now
- [ ] Add immediate next task

## Upcoming
- [ ] Add next milestone or task group
````

## `dev/10_design.md`
### Purpose
Record the current stable architecture, design assumptions, and capability boundaries.

### Recommended template
````markdown
# Design

Last updated: YYYY-MM-DD

## Purpose
This document records the current stable architecture, operating assumptions, and capability boundaries for the repository.

## Document map
- Plan: `dev/05_plan.md`
- Schemas: `dev/40_schemas.md`
- Decisions: `dev/decisions/`
- Instructions: `dev/instructions/`
- Sessions: `dev/sessions/`

## Current design
Add stable architecture and design notes here.

## Open questions
- [ ] Add unresolved design questions here
````

## `dev/40_schemas.md`
### Purpose
Document structural contracts used by the repository.

### Recommended template
````markdown
# Schemas

Last updated: YYYY-MM-DD

## Purpose
This document records important structural contracts used by the repository, including data objects, files, tables, configuration structures, and other interfaces whose shape must remain explicit.

## How to use
- Add schemas for any durable data structures that other code depends on.
- Record required fields, types, constraints, and invariants where relevant.
- Update this file when new structured artifacts are introduced or existing ones change.

## Schemas
### Example schema
| Field | Type | Required | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| id | character | yes | Stable identifier |
````

## `dev/decisions/README.md`
### Purpose
Explain the role of the decisions directory.

### Recommended template
````markdown
# Decision Records

This directory stores durable design and architecture decisions.

Use this directory for decisions that:
- materially affect future work
- involve tradeoffs or alternatives
- should remain easy to review over time

Do not use this directory for task tracking or transient session notes.
````

## `dev/instructions/development-governance.md`
Installed from the package’s canonical source file in:

```text
inst/instructions/development-governance.md
```

This file should not be dynamically customized.

---

# Overwrite behavior

## Default behavior
With:

```r
overwrite = FALSE
```

the handler must:
- create missing files and directories
- preserve existing governance files
- preserve any user edits
- report which files were skipped because they already existed

## Overwrite behavior
With:

```r
overwrite = TRUE
```

the handler may replace:
- `dev/05_plan.md`
- `dev/10_design.md`
- `dev/40_schemas.md`
- `dev/decisions/README.md`
- `dev/instructions/development-governance.md`

but should not:
- delete user-created files
- remove directories
- overwrite unrelated files outside the handler’s scope

This keeps the behavior transparent and bounded.

---

# Result object

The handler should return a lightweight structured result, preferably a named list.

## Recommended fields
```r
list(
  module_name = "development-governance",
  instruction_source = "<path to inst/instructions/development-governance.md>",
  instruction_target = "<target>/dev/instructions/development-governance.md",
  dirs_created = c(...),
  files_written = c(...),
  files_skipped = c(...),
  warnings = c(...),
  next_steps = c(...)
)
```

## Recommended `next_steps`
Examples:
- review `dev/05_plan.md`
- review `dev/10_design.md`
- review `dev/40_schemas.md`
- add repo-specific design decisions under `dev/decisions/`
- use the installed development-governance instructions in future chat sessions

This makes the result object useful both for interactive messaging and for future testing.

---

# Messaging behavior

The handler should emit clear, `usethis`-style progress messages.

## Recommended examples
- created `dev/`
- created `dev/decisions/`
- wrote `dev/05_plan.md`
- wrote `dev/10_design.md`
- wrote `dev/40_schemas.md`
- left existing `dev/05_plan.md` unchanged
- installed `dev/instructions/development-governance.md`

Then provide a concise final summary.

Avoid excessive verbosity.

---

# Validation and error handling

## Error if:
- `path` does not exist
- `path` is not a directory
- the package source instruction file cannot be found

## Warn, but do not error, if:
- the target repo lacks package markers such as `DESCRIPTION` or `.Rproj`

Reason:
- development-governance may be useful in more than one repo type
- scoping can remain broader than R packages, even if R packages are the main use case

---

# Ownership boundary

This handler is responsible for:
- installing the module instruction text
- scaffolding the governance structure
- creating starter template files

This handler is **not** responsible for:
- writing substantive repo-specific architecture
- writing repo-specific plans
- generating actual decision records
- replacing the chat session’s role in providing paste-ready content during real project work

That boundary is essential to the overall design.

---

# Tests

## Required tests

### 1. installs instruction file
- creates `dev/instructions/development-governance.md`

### 2. scaffolds required directories
- creates `dev/`
- creates `dev/decisions/`
- creates `dev/instructions/`
- creates `dev/sessions/`

### 3. scaffolds required files
- creates `dev/05_plan.md`
- creates `dev/10_design.md`
- creates `dev/40_schemas.md`
- creates `dev/decisions/README.md`

### 4. preserves existing files when `overwrite = FALSE`
- existing content remains unchanged

### 5. overwrites owned files when `overwrite = TRUE`
- scaffold-owned files are replaced

### 6. returns structured result
- result contains expected named fields
- result reports created vs skipped files appropriately

## Nice-to-have tests
- emitted messages are informative
- the installed instruction file content matches the package source file

---

# pkgdown documentation recommendation

Document `module_development_governance()` as a repo-governance utility.

## Key points to emphasize
- it installs the `development-governance` instruction module
- it scaffolds the internal `dev/` governance framework
- it does not write repo-specific substantive content
- chat sessions remain responsible for producing paste-ready updates to these governance artifacts during real development work

This distinction should be explicit in reference docs and any governance vignette.

---

# Relationship to `use_instructions()`

Under the refactored handler architecture, this function is called by:

```r
use_instructions(...)
```

when the user requests the public module:

```text
development-governance
```

This means the normal user-facing workflow remains:

```r
use_instructions(c("chat-manual", "goals", "development-governance"))
```

while the module handler encapsulates the installation and scaffolding details.

---

# Recommendation

This spec is now concrete enough to serve as the first implementation target in the handler-based refactor.

## Best next step
I recommend next defining one of the following:

1. **`validate_dev_governance()` spec**, to complete the governance capability, or  
2. **the code-level refactor plan** for migrating existing modules and `use_instructions()` to the handler architecture

I recommend **option 2 next** if your goal is implementation sequencing, and **option 1 next** if your goal is capability completeness.

## Copilot

Great — here is the **code-level refactor plan** for migrating `reproducibleai` to the handler-based instruction architecture.

This is focused on implementation sequencing, boundaries, and minimizing disruption.

---

# Refactor goal

Refactor `reproducibleai` from a primarily file-copy-based instruction installer to a **handler-based instruction architecture** in which:

- canonical module text remains static markdown in `inst/instructions/`
- every public module has a corresponding handler function
- `use_instructions()` becomes a simple orchestrator that calls module handlers
- some handlers remain simple install-only wrappers
- other handlers additionally perform repo configuration/scaffolding

This preserves your instruction-first design while enabling configuration-aware modules.

---

# High-level migration strategy

I recommend a **4-phase migration**:

1. **stabilize the new architecture contract**
2. **refactor existing modules to handler-backed installation**
3. **refactor `use_instructions()` to orchestrate handlers**
4. **add configuration-aware handlers, starting with development-governance**

This reduces risk and avoids trying to change everything at once.

---

# Phase 1 — establish the architecture contract

## Objective
Define the internal conventions that the refactor will rely on.

## Deliverables
- module naming convention finalized
- source instruction location finalized: `inst/instructions/`
- handler naming rule finalized: `module_<module_name>()`
- handler responsibilities finalized:
  - install static text
  - perform optional module-specific config
- result object structure finalized
- public UX preserved:
  - `instructions_available()`
  - `instructions_recipes()`
  - `use_instructions()`

## Implementation notes
This phase is mostly design/organization, but may include:
- internal helper stubs
- comments/docs describing the new architecture
- maybe an internal module registry derived from available source files, if needed

## Recommendation
Do this first so later code work has clear conventions.

---

# Phase 2 — create handler functions for all existing modules

## Objective
Ensure every existing public module has a handler, even if the config portion is empty.

## Existing public modules likely include
From the current README example:
- `chat-manual`
- `goals`
- `python-package`
- `quarto-book`
- `r-package`
- `shiny-golem`
- `user-manual` citeturn0commentaryto=multi_tool_use.parallel1

Plus the new modules:
- `development-governance`
- `parameterized-help`

## Deliverables
Create handler functions such as:
- `module_chat_manual()`
- `module_goals()`
- `module_python_package()`
- `module_quarto_book()`
- `module_r_package()`
- `module_shiny_golem()`
- `module_user_manual()`
- `module_development_governance()`
- `module_parameterized_help()`

## Behavior in this phase
For most existing modules, handlers can initially do only:
1. install static instruction text
2. return a result object

That is enough to standardize the architecture without introducing too much change.

## Internal helpers recommended
Create shared internals for:
- mapping public module name to source file path
- mapping public module name to target file path
- copying module text with overwrite handling
- building result objects

This will keep simple handlers very short.

---

# Phase 3 — refactor `use_instructions()` to call handlers

## Objective
Move orchestration responsibility into `use_instructions()` while removing direct module-file-copy assumptions.

## Deliverables
Refactor `use_instructions()` so it:
1. accepts module vectors or recipe outputs
2. normalizes and deduplicates module names
3. validates modules
4. maps module names to handler functions
5. calls handlers in order
6. aggregates results
7. prints a summary
8. returns a structured object

## Important compatibility goal
The user-facing workflow should not change materially.

This should still work:
```r
recipes <- instructions_recipes()
use_instructions(recipes$r_package)
```

## Migration note
During this phase, `use_instructions()` can rely on all handlers already existing from Phase 2.

That is why Phase 2 should come first.

---

# Phase 4 — activate meaningful config behavior in selected handlers

## Objective
Start using the power of the new architecture where it matters most.

## First priority
Implement full config behavior in:
- `module_development_governance()`

This becomes the first genuine “instruction + config” handler.

## Second priority
Implement full config behavior in:
- `module_parameterized_help()`

once the governance layer is stable.

## Possible later candidates
Potentially:
- `module_shiny_golem()`
- `module_r_package()`

if you later find clear setup needs that belong there.

## Recommendation
Do not make every module complex just because the architecture allows it.

Keep complexity only where it adds real value.

---

# Recommended package structure changes

## Keep
- `inst/instructions/` as the home for canonical instruction markdown files

## Add/expand in `R/`
You will likely want:
- one file for `use_instructions()`
- one or more files for module handlers
- one file for internal helper utilities

### Example organization
```text
R/
  instructions-use.R
  instructions-handlers-core.R
  instructions-handlers-basic.R
  instructions-handlers-governance.R
  instructions-handlers-shiny.R
  instructions-internal.R
```

This is just one possible layout, but some separation will help.

---

# Recommended internal helper functions

To avoid duplication, I strongly recommend internal helpers.

## Core internal helpers
### `module_source_path(module_name)`
Return the canonical source path in `inst/instructions/`.

### `module_target_path(module_name, path)`
Return the target repo path under `dev/instructions/`.

### `install_module_text(module_name, path, overwrite = FALSE)`
Copy the canonical markdown file into the target repo.

This should handle:
- ensuring `dev/instructions/` exists
- overwrite logic
- returning details about written/skipped files

### `module_name_to_handler(module_name)`
Convert:
- `development-governance`
to:
- `module_development_governance`

### `get_module_handler(module_name)`
Resolve the actual function object or error clearly.

### `new_module_result(...)`
Construct a standard result object.

### `combine_module_results(results)`
Aggregate per-handler results for `use_instructions()`.

These helpers will make the codebase much easier to maintain.

---

# Refactor sequencing in more detail

## Step 1
Add internal helper utilities first.

Why:
- reduces duplication
- makes handlers trivial to write

## Step 2
Implement simple handlers for all current modules.

These handlers mostly wrap:
- `install_module_text()`

## Step 3
Refactor `instructions_available()`, if needed, to stay aligned with the handler-based system.

### Recommendation
It can still be based on canonical instruction files in `inst/instructions/`, but you may also want a check that handlers exist for all listed modules.

## Step 4
Refactor `use_instructions()` to use handler dispatch.

## Step 5
Add tests ensuring old workflows still behave properly.

## Step 6
Implement full `module_development_governance()` config behavior.

## Step 7
Add governance-specific tests and docs.

## Step 8
Implement `module_parameterized_help()` after governance is stable.

---

# Backward compatibility considerations

## Preserve existing public API where possible
Do not unnecessarily rename:
- `instructions_available()`
- `instructions_recipes()`
- `use_instructions()`

That continuity matters.

## Preserve recipe semantics
Recipes should remain simple vectors of public module names.

## Preserve static instruction reviewability
Do not move canonical module text into R code.

## Change internal implementation, not external philosophy
This should feel like an internal strengthening of the system, not a reinvention.

That is important for user trust.

---

# Testing strategy for the refactor

## A. Architecture tests
- every available module has a corresponding handler
- every handler can locate its source instruction file
- module name ↔ handler name mapping works

## B. `use_instructions()` tests
- handlers are called in order
- duplicate modules are deduplicated
- results are aggregated correctly
- unknown modules error clearly
- missing handlers error clearly

## C. Existing behavior preservation tests
- existing recipes still install expected instruction files
- static module text deployed to target repos remains unchanged relative to canonical sources

## D. Config-aware handler tests
Start with:
- `module_development_governance()`

These tests should verify scaffold behavior thoroughly.

---

# Documentation updates needed during the refactor

## Update README / quick start if necessary
If the user-facing workflow does not change, README changes may be small.

Mostly likely additions:
- explain that modules are installed through handlers internally
- reassure users that canonical instruction text remains static and reviewable

## Add pkgdown/reference docs
For:
- `module_development_governance()`
- later `module_parameterized_help()`

## Add design/governance docs in the package itself
You already started this work with governance and parameterized-help design artifacts; the refactor should now also be captured in:
- `dev/10_design.md`
- probably a decision record

because this is a meaningful architecture decision.

---

# Recommended implementation order in practical terms

If I were sequencing the actual coding work, I would do it in this order:

## 1. internal helpers
- module path resolution
- text installation
- result construction
- handler resolution

## 2. simple handlers for all current modules
No config logic yet beyond installation.

## 3. `use_instructions()` refactor
Dispatch to handlers.

## 4. tests for orchestration and handler coverage

## 5. `module_development_governance()` full scaffold logic
Including:
- `dev/05_plan.md`
- `dev/10_design.md`
- `dev/40_schemas.md`
- `dev/decisions/`
- `dev/instructions/`
- `dev/sessions/`

## 6. tests for development-governance scaffold

## 7. pkgdown/reference docs for governance handler

## 8. only then move on to parameterized-help handler implementation

That is the cleanest path.

---

# Key implementation principle

I recommend one explicit guiding rule for the refactor:

> **Handlers may add operational support, but they must not obscure or redefine the canonical static instruction content.**

That should remain a non-negotiable invariant.

---

# Strong recommendation

This refactor plan is strong because it:
- preserves your most valued UX and reviewability properties
- keeps static instructions canonical
- simplifies `use_instructions()` conceptually
- gives you a clean place to add governance and help-specific scaffolding
- minimizes risk by using phased migration

---

## Best next step
The most logical next step is to draft the **internal helper function specs** first, because they are the foundation for both:
- the handler refactor
- and the updated `use_instructions()` orchestration

If you want, I can draft those helper specs next.

Concur. Proceed. 

## Copilot

Great — here is the **internal helper function specification** for the handler-based refactor.

These helpers are designed to keep:

- module handlers very small
- `use_instructions()` simple
- behavior consistent across all modules

They are internal implementation utilities, not user-facing API.

---

# Internal helper function spec

## Design goal
The helper layer should provide a minimal toolkit for:

1. locating canonical instruction source files
2. determining target install paths
3. installing static module text
4. resolving handler names/functions
5. creating consistent result objects
6. aggregating results for `use_instructions()`

These helpers should keep most module handlers to a simple pattern like:

```r
module_<name>() {
  # install static text
  # do optional module-specific config
  # return structured result
}
```

---

# 1. `module_source_path()`

## Purpose
Return the canonical package source path for a module’s static instruction markdown file in `inst/instructions/`.

## Signature
```r
module_source_path(module_name)
```

## Input
### `module_name`
Character scalar public module name in kebab-case, e.g.:
```r
"development-governance"
```

## Behavior
Return the package-installed path to:

```text
inst/instructions/<module_name>.md
```

using package-safe path resolution.

For example:
```text
inst/instructions/development-governance.md
```

## Error behavior
Error if:
- `module_name` is missing or not scalar character
- the source file does not exist

## Output
Character scalar path.

## Notes
This helper should be the single source of truth for where canonical module text lives.

---

# 2. `module_target_path()`

## Purpose
Return the target repository path where a module’s instruction file should be installed.

## Signature
```r
module_target_path(module_name, path = ".")
```

## Inputs
### `module_name`
Character scalar public module name in kebab-case.

### `path`
Target repo root.

## Behavior
Return:

```text
<path>/dev/instructions/<module_name>.md
```

For example:
```text
./dev/instructions/development-governance.md
```

## Error behavior
Error if:
- `module_name` invalid
- `path` invalid

## Output
Character scalar path.

## Notes
This helper centralizes install location logic.

---

# 3. `ensure_dir()`

## Purpose
Ensure a directory exists, creating it recursively if necessary, and report whether it was created or already existed.

## Signature
```r
ensure_dir(dir_path)
```

## Input
### `dir_path`
Character scalar path to a directory.

## Behavior
- create directory recursively if missing
- do nothing if already exists

## Output
Return a small structured list like:
```r
list(
  path = dir_path,
  created = TRUE/FALSE
)
```

## Error behavior
Error if:
- path exists but is not a directory
- directory creation fails

## Notes
This helper will be reused heavily by config-aware handlers.

---

# 4. `install_module_text()`

## Purpose
Install a module’s canonical static instruction markdown into the target repository’s `dev/instructions/` directory.

## Signature
```r
install_module_text(module_name, path = ".", overwrite = FALSE)
```

## Inputs
### `module_name`
Public module name in kebab-case.

### `path`
Target repo root.

### `overwrite`
Logical; whether to replace an existing installed file.

## Behavior
1. locate source file via `module_source_path()`
2. ensure target directory `dev/instructions/` exists
3. compute target file path via `module_target_path()`
4. write/copy the file if:
   - target missing, or
   - `overwrite = TRUE`
5. preserve existing file when:
   - target exists and `overwrite = FALSE`

## Output
Return a structured list like:
```r
list(
  module_name = module_name,
  source = <source path>,
  target = <target path>,
  dirs_created = c(...),
  files_written = c(...),
  files_skipped = c(...)
)
```

## Error behavior
Error if:
- source file missing
- target root invalid
- copy/write fails

## Notes
This helper should be the standard Part A implementation in nearly every module handler.

---

# 5. `module_name_to_handler()`

## Purpose
Map a public module name in kebab-case to its handler function name in snake_case.

## Signature
```r
module_name_to_handler(module_name)
```

## Input
### `module_name`
Character scalar public module name, e.g.:
```r
"parameterized-help"
```

## Behavior
Transform:
- replace `-` with `_`
- prepend `module_`

Example:
```r
"parameterized-help" -> "module_parameterized_help"
```

## Output
Character scalar function name.

## Error behavior
Error if:
- invalid module name input

## Notes
This should be a pure deterministic mapping.

---

# 6. `get_module_handler()`

## Purpose
Resolve a module’s handler function object from its public module name.

## Signature
```r
get_module_handler(module_name)
```

## Input
### `module_name`
Character scalar public module name.

## Behavior
1. compute handler name using `module_name_to_handler()`
2. resolve that function in the package namespace/internal environment
3. return the function object

## Output
Function object.

## Error behavior
Error if:
- handler name cannot be resolved
- resolved object is not a function

## Notes
This helper makes `use_instructions()` clean and centralizes handler-resolution errors.

---

# 7. `new_module_result()`

## Purpose
Construct a standard structured result object for a module handler.

## Signature
```r
new_module_result(
  module_name,
  instruction_source = character(),
  instruction_target = character(),
  dirs_created = character(),
  files_written = character(),
  files_skipped = character(),
  warnings = character(),
  next_steps = character()
)
```

## Inputs
Fields of the result object.

## Behavior
Return a named list with a standard shape.

## Output
Named list.

## Notes
Using this helper will enforce consistency across handlers.

---

# 8. `merge_module_results()` or `combine_module_results()`

## Purpose
Aggregate per-module handler results into one summary object for `use_instructions()`.

## Signature
```r
combine_module_results(results)
```

## Input
### `results`
List of handler result objects.

## Behavior
Aggregate fields such as:
- modules processed
- dirs created
- files written
- files skipped
- warnings
- next steps

## Output
Named list like:
```r
list(
  modules_processed = c(...),
  files_written = c(...),
  files_skipped = c(...),
  dirs_created = c(...),
  warnings = c(...),
  next_steps = c(...),
  results = results
)
```

## Notes
This is the return backbone for `use_instructions()`.

---

# 9. `normalize_module_names()`

## Purpose
Validate, normalize, and deduplicate user-supplied module vectors while preserving order.

## Signature
```r
normalize_module_names(modules)
```

## Input
### `modules`
Character vector of public module names.

## Behavior
- ensure input is character and non-empty
- trim/validate format if needed
- remove duplicates, preserving first occurrence
- return normalized ordered vector

## Output
Character vector.

## Error behavior
Error if:
- input is empty
- input is not character
- any element invalid/missing

## Notes
This helper should be called early in `use_instructions()`.

---

# 10. `validate_modules_available()`

## Purpose
Ensure all requested module names are known/available before attempting handler resolution.

## Signature
```r
validate_modules_available(modules, available = instructions_available())
```

## Inputs
### `modules`
Requested modules.

### `available`
Available module names.

## Behavior
- compare requested vs available
- error if any are unknown

## Output
Return invisibly `TRUE` or normalized vector if valid.

## Error behavior
Error clearly listing:
- unknown modules
- available modules

## Notes
This should happen before handler invocation.

---

# 11. `summarize_use_instructions()`

## Purpose
Print a concise human-readable summary of `use_instructions()` execution.

## Signature
```r
summarize_use_instructions(result)
```

## Input
### `result`
Combined result object from `combine_module_results()`.

## Behavior
Print concise summary including:
- modules installed
- files written/skipped
- directories created
- warnings
- next steps

## Output
Primarily side-effect printing; may invisibly return `result`.

## Notes
Keeping summary logic separate will keep `use_instructions()` cleaner.

---

# Recommended implementation pattern in handlers

With these helpers, a simple handler can look conceptually like:

```r
module_goals <- function(path = ".", overwrite = FALSE, ...) {
  install <- install_module_text("goals", path = path, overwrite = overwrite)

  new_module_result(
    module_name = "goals",
    instruction_source = install$source,
    instruction_target = install$target,
    dirs_created = install$dirs_created,
    files_written = install$files_written,
    files_skipped = install$files_skipped
  )
}
```

And a config-aware handler like `module_development_governance()` can:
- call `install_module_text()`
- call `ensure_dir()` multiple times
- write template files
- combine everything into `new_module_result()`

That is the exact simplification we want.

---

# Recommended implementation priority for helpers

I would implement these first:

## Priority 1
- `module_source_path()`
- `module_target_path()`
- `ensure_dir()`
- `install_module_text()`

These are needed by every handler.

## Priority 2
- `module_name_to_handler()`
- `get_module_handler()`
- `normalize_module_names()`
- `validate_modules_available()`

These support `use_instructions()` orchestration.

## Priority 3
- `new_module_result()`
- `combine_module_results()`
- `summarize_use_instructions()`

These support consistency and reporting.

That sequence mirrors the refactor plan well.

---

# Testing recommendations for helpers

## `module_source_path()`
- returns correct source path for known module
- errors for missing source file

## `module_target_path()`
- returns correct target path

## `ensure_dir()`
- creates missing directory
- returns `created = FALSE` when already exists
- errors when path exists as file

## `install_module_text()`
- installs source file to target repo
- creates `dev/instructions/` if needed
- skips existing file when `overwrite = FALSE`
- replaces existing file when `overwrite = TRUE`

## `module_name_to_handler()`
- maps hyphenated names correctly

## `get_module_handler()`
- resolves valid handler
- errors on missing handler

## `normalize_module_names()`
- deduplicates preserving order
- errors on empty input

## `combine_module_results()`
- aggregates lists correctly

These tests will make the refactor much safer.

---

# Strong recommendation

This helper layer is the right foundation because it:
- keeps canonical instructions static
- keeps handlers concise
- keeps `use_instructions()` simple
- centralizes path, install, and summary logic
- gives you a clean base for both governance and parameterized-help capabilities

---

The most logical next step is now to draft the **simple handler template pattern** and then the **refactored `use_instructions()` algorithm spec** in near-implementation form.

Since we already outlined `use_instructions()` conceptually, I recommend next drafting:
- the near-final pseudocode/spec for `use_instructions()`
- and the pattern for “simple handlers” vs “config-aware handlers”

If you want, I can do that next.

Concur. Proceed. 

## Copilot

Great — here is the **near-implementation spec** for:

1. the **simple-handler pattern**
2. the **config-aware handler pattern**
3. the **refactored `use_instructions()` algorithm**

This should bring the architecture close to code without prematurely locking you into exact implementation syntax.

---

# 1. Simple handler pattern

## Purpose
A **simple handler** is for modules whose only current responsibility is:

- install the canonical static instruction text into the target repo
- return a standard result object

These modules do **not** currently require repo-specific scaffolding or configuration.

Examples likely include:
- `chat-manual`
- `goals`
- `python-package`
- `quarto-book`
- `r-package`
- `user-manual`

Possibly also `shiny-golem` initially, unless you decide it needs config behavior now.

---

## Required behavior
A simple handler must:

1. call `install_module_text()`
2. construct a standard result using `new_module_result()`
3. return that result

It should **not**:
- add custom file creation
- perform unexpected validation
- do repo-specific mutations beyond instruction installation

---

## Conceptual pattern

```r
module_<name> <- function(path = ".", overwrite = FALSE, ...) {
  install <- install_module_text(
    module_name = "<public-module-name>",
    path = path,
    overwrite = overwrite
  )

  new_module_result(
    module_name = "<public-module-name>",
    instruction_source = install$source,
    instruction_target = install$target,
    dirs_created = install$dirs_created,
    files_written = install$files_written,
    files_skipped = install$files_skipped
  )
}
```

---

## Example conceptual implementation

```r
module_goals <- function(path = ".", overwrite = FALSE, ...) {
  install <- install_module_text(
    module_name = "goals",
    path = path,
    overwrite = overwrite
  )

  new_module_result(
    module_name = "goals",
    instruction_source = install$source,
    instruction_target = install$target,
    dirs_created = install$dirs_created,
    files_written = install$files_written,
    files_skipped = install$files_skipped
  )
}
```

---

## Design benefit
This pattern means most modules stay:
- tiny
- readable
- consistent
- easy to review

which aligns strongly with your design goals.

---

# 2. Config-aware handler pattern

## Purpose
A **config-aware handler** is for modules that need more than just instruction installation.

These modules:
- install the canonical static instruction text
- scaffold or validate repo structure needed to support the module
- return a standard result object including those additional actions

Examples:
- `development-governance`
- later `parameterized-help`

---

## Required behavior
A config-aware handler must:

1. call `install_module_text()`
2. perform module-specific setup/config
3. collect created/skipped files and directories
4. construct a standard result with all actions included
5. return that result

---

## Conceptual pattern

```r
module_<name> <- function(path = ".", overwrite = FALSE, ...) {
  install <- install_module_text(
    module_name = "<public-module-name>",
    path = path,
    overwrite = overwrite
  )

  # module-specific setup
  # ensure dirs
  # write templates
  # gather warnings / next steps

  new_module_result(
    module_name = "<public-module-name>",
    instruction_source = install$source,
    instruction_target = install$target,
    dirs_created = c(install$dirs_created, ...),
    files_written = c(install$files_written, ...),
    files_skipped = c(install$files_skipped, ...),
    warnings = c(...),
    next_steps = c(...)
  )
}
```

---

## Example conceptual role for `module_development_governance()`

It should:

### install
- `development-governance.md`

### ensure
- `dev/`
- `dev/decisions/`
- `dev/instructions/`
- `dev/sessions/`

### create
- `dev/05_plan.md`
- `dev/10_design.md`
- `dev/40_schemas.md`
- `dev/decisions/README.md`

### return
- full result object with created/skipped paths and next steps

---

## Design rule
A config-aware handler may add repo setup, but it must **not** rewrite or customize the canonical instruction text.

That constraint should remain absolute.

---

# 3. Refactored `use_instructions()` algorithm

## Purpose
`use_instructions()` remains the primary public entry point for installing instruction modules and recipes into a target repo.

Under the refactor, it becomes a thin orchestrator over module handlers.

---

## Recommended signature

```r
use_instructions(
  modules,
  path = ".",
  overwrite = FALSE,
  ...
)
```

---

## High-level flow

### Step 1 — validate input
- ensure `modules` is supplied
- ensure `modules` is a character vector or recipe output equivalent

### Step 2 — normalize module names
Call:
- `normalize_module_names(modules)`

This should:
- validate type
- deduplicate while preserving order

### Step 3 — validate availability
Call:
- `validate_modules_available(modules)`

This should confirm every requested module exists in `instructions_available()`.

### Step 4 — resolve handlers
For each module:
- call `get_module_handler(module_name)`

### Step 5 — execute handlers in order
For each module in normalized order:
- print progress message
- call handler with `path`, `overwrite`, `...`
- collect result

### Step 6 — combine results
Call:
- `combine_module_results(results)`

### Step 7 — print summary
Call:
- `summarize_use_instructions(combined_result)`

### Step 8 — return structured result
Return the combined result object, invisibly or visibly depending on your style preference.

I’d recommend returning it visibly in development, invisibly for normal usage. Either is defensible.

---

# Pseudocode sketch

```r
use_instructions <- function(modules, path = ".", overwrite = FALSE, ...) {
  modules <- normalize_module_names(modules)
  validate_modules_available(modules)

  results <- vector("list", length(modules))
  names(results) <- modules

  cli_message("Installing instruction modules into {path}")

  for (i in seq_along(modules)) {
    module_name <- modules[[i]]
    handler <- get_module_handler(module_name)

    cli_message("[{i}/{length(modules)}] Installing module: {module_name}")

    results[[i]] <- handler(
      path = path,
      overwrite = overwrite,
      ...
    )
  }

  combined <- combine_module_results(results)
  summarize_use_instructions(combined)

  invisible(combined)
}
```

This is just conceptual, but it captures the intended structure well.

---

# 4. Behavior rules for `use_instructions()`

## It should do
- orchestrate handlers
- preserve recipe order
- deduplicate repeated modules
- stop clearly on invalid modules or missing handlers
- aggregate actions and next steps

## It should not do
- install module text directly
- contain module-specific config logic
- special-case development-governance or parameterized-help
- dynamically edit canonical module text

Those responsibilities belong elsewhere.

---

# 5. Progress messaging pattern

## Recommended pattern

### Start
```text
Installing instruction modules into <path>
```

### Per module
```text
[1/3] Installing module: goals
[2/3] Installing module: r-package
[3/3] Installing module: development-governance
```

### End
```text
Done. Installed 3 instruction modules.
Created 4 directories and 6 files.
Next steps:
- review dev/10_design.md
- review dev/40_schemas.md
```

This should feel like `usethis`:
- clear
- calm
- not noisy

---

# 6. Aggregation rules

`combine_module_results()` should aggregate across handlers:

## Aggregate fields
- `modules_processed`
- `dirs_created`
- `files_written`
- `files_skipped`
- `warnings`
- `next_steps`
- `results`

## Recommended behavior
- preserve module order
- deduplicate repeated warnings/next steps if needed
- keep raw per-module results nested for inspection

That gives both human and programmatic value.

---

# 7. Error behavior

## `use_instructions()` should error if:
- no modules provided
- requested module unknown
- handler missing
- handler errors during execution
- target path invalid

## Recommendation for v1
Fail fast.

Do **not** try to continue past failed module installation unless you later introduce an explicit argument like:
- `stop_on_error = FALSE`

For now, fail-fast is simpler and safer.

---

# 8. Migration rule for existing modules

All current modules should first be converted into **simple handlers**.

That means the refactor path is:

1. create simple handlers for all existing modules
2. update `use_instructions()` to call handlers
3. only then add richer config-aware behavior to selected modules

This makes the migration much safer than trying to convert behavior and orchestration simultaneously.

---

# 9. Recommended file organization in `R/`

I recommend splitting implementation like this:

## `instructions-use.R`
- `use_instructions()`

## `instructions-helpers.R`
- `module_source_path()`
- `module_target_path()`
- `ensure_dir()`
- `install_module_text()`
- `module_name_to_handler()`
- `get_module_handler()`
- `new_module_result()`
- `combine_module_results()`
- `normalize_module_names()`
- `validate_modules_available()`
- `summarize_use_instructions()`

## `instructions-handlers-basic.R`
- simple handlers for existing modules

## `instructions-handlers-governance.R`
- `module_development_governance()`

## `instructions-handlers-shiny.R`
- later `module_parameterized_help()`

This is not mandatory, but I think it will age well.

---

# 10. Recommended test layering

## Layer 1 — helpers
Test helper functions independently.

## Layer 2 — handler contract
Test:
- every module has a handler
- simple handlers install correct text
- config-aware handlers scaffold expected artifacts

## Layer 3 — orchestration
Test:
- `use_instructions()` calls handlers in order
- duplicate modules only run once
- results are aggregated correctly

This layered testing strategy is important because the refactor changes internal architecture while preserving public UX.

---

# 11. Strong recommendation

This design is strong because it gives you:

- static, reviewable instruction content
- consistent module execution
- clean separation between orchestration and module logic
- minimal complexity for simple modules
- extensibility for governance and parameterized-help

without giving up the simplicity and trustworthiness of your current model.

---

## Best next step
The most logical next move is to define the **implementation checklist / task plan** for executing this refactor in `reproducibleai`.

That would turn the architecture into an actionable development sequence suitable for:
- `dev/05_plan.md`
- maybe `dev/10_design.md`
- and immediate coding work

If you want, I can draft that implementation checklist next.

Concur. Proceed.

  - DoD:

    - Helper behavior is covered by unit tests for normal and failure cases.

- [ ] A2: Define standard handler contract and result object structure
  - DoD:

### Milestone B — migrate existing modules to handlers

  - DoD:

    - Simple handlers install canonical static module text from `inst/instructions/`.
    - Existing instruction content remains unchanged.
  - Artifacts:
    - `R/instructions-handlers-basic.R`
    - handler coverage tests

- [ ] B2: Add architecture test to verify every available module has a handler

- [ ] D2: Document development-governance capability in package docs

### Milestone E — stabilize and validate refactor

````markdown name=dev/10_design.md
# Design

Last updated: 2026-05-30

## Purpose
This document records the current stable architecture, operating assumptions, and capability boundaries for the repository.

## Document map
- Plan: `dev/05_plan.md`
- Schemas: `dev/40_schemas.md`
- Decisions: `dev/decisions/`
- Instructions: `dev/instructions/`
- Sessions: `dev/sessions/`

## Current design

### Instruction-first architecture
`reproducibleai` remains instruction-first.

The primary user-facing workflow is still:
- discover available instruction modules
- compose modules as a recipe
- install them into a target repository with `use_instructions()`

Canonical instruction text remains static, natural-language markdown stored in `inst/instructions/`. These files are intended to remain:
- easy to review
- easy to diff in Git
- readable by non-programmers
- stable across team repositories

### Handler-based installation model
Each public instruction module has a corresponding handler function named:

- `module_<module_name>()`

where public module names remain kebab-case and handler names use snake_case.

Examples:
- `development-governance` -> `module_development_governance()`
- `parameterized-help` -> `module_parameterized_help()`

Each module handler has two responsibilities:
1. install the module’s canonical static instruction text into `dev/instructions/`
2. perform any module-specific configuration required to support the module in the target repository

If a module has no additional configuration needs, the configuration step is a no-op, but the handler still exists.

### Canonical source and target locations
Canonical source instruction files are stored in:

- `inst/instructions/`

Installed instruction files are written to the target repository under:

- `dev/instructions/`

Handlers must not rewrite or dynamically customize the substantive wording of canonical instruction text.

### `use_instructions()` orchestration role
`use_instructions()` is the high-level orchestrator for instruction installation.

Its role is to:
- accept module names or recipe outputs
- normalize and validate requested modules
- resolve handler functions
- invoke handlers in order
- aggregate and summarize results

`use_instructions()` should not contain module-specific setup logic beyond generic orchestration.

### Module categories
The architecture supports two practical module categories:

#### Simple handlers
These modules install static instruction text and return a standard result object.

Examples include:
- `chat-manual`
- `goals`
- `r-package`

#### Config-aware handlers
These modules install static instruction text and also scaffold or validate supporting repository structure.

The first config-aware module is:

- `development-governance`

A later config-aware module is expected for:

- `parameterized-help`

### Development-governance capability
The `development-governance` module is a first-class package capability.

Its handler scaffolds the minimum governance framework required for durable AI-assisted development:

- `dev/05_plan.md`
- `dev/10_design.md`
- `dev/40_schemas.md`
- `dev/decisions/`
- `dev/instructions/`
- `dev/sessions/`

`dev/40_schemas.md` is required, not optional. Structural contracts are critical in data-science-oriented repositories and should be documented explicitly.

### Design invariants
The following are current architectural invariants:

- Canonical instruction text remains static in `inst/instructions/`.
- Every public instruction module has a corresponding handler function.
- Handlers install static instruction text and may perform config.
- Handlers do not rewrite substantive instruction text.
- `use_instructions()` remains the main public entry point.
- Recipes remain simple compositions of public module names.
- `dev/40_schemas.md` is a required development-governance artifact.

## Open questions
- [ ] Finalize helper function implementations and test structure for the handler refactor.
- [ ] Decide whether handler result objects should be returned visibly or invisibly from `use_instructions()`.
- [ ] Decide how `instructions_available()` should validate consistency between static module files and implemented handlers.
````

If you want, next I can draft the paste-ready `dev/40_schemas.md` update for the new handler/result-object architecture.

## Copilot

Yes — the current `dev/10_design.md` is a good start, but it needs to be updated to reflect the major architectural clarification we made:

- `reproducibleai` remains **instruction-first**
- modules remain **static markdown in `inst/instructions/`**
- **every** module now has a handler function
- handlers install static text and optionally perform config
- `use_instructions()` becomes a **handler orchestrator**
- `dev/40_schemas.md` is **required**, not optional

````markdown name=dev/10_design.md url=https://github.com/MVR-GIS/reproducibleai/blob/079792f7d02e00ab084f3cbb8a146013041d5ff0/dev/10_design.md
## Instruction system architecture

### Purpose
`reproducibleai` is an instruction-first R package for supporting reproducible AI-assisted development workflows.

The package is designed to help teams:
- define reusable modular chat instructions
- compose instructions into recipes
- install those instructions consistently across repositories
- support selected instruction modules with configuration-aware setup behavior where needed

The package must preserve a workflow that is easy to review, easy to diff, and understandable to collaborators who are not software developers.

### Core design principles
The instruction system is based on the following principles:

- Instruction modules are defined as static natural-language markdown files.
- Canonical instruction text must remain easy to review by non-programmers.
- Recipes should remain simple compositions of module names.
- Version changes to instruction text should remain clear in Git diffs.
- Installed instructions should be consistent across team repositories.
- Operational support may be added through handler functions, but handlers must not rewrite the substantive wording of canonical instruction text.

### Canonical instruction source
Canonical instruction module files are stored in:

- `inst/instructions/`

These files are the authoritative reviewed instruction texts for the package.

Examples include:
- `inst/instructions/development-governance.md`
- `inst/instructions/parameterized-help.md`

### Installed instruction destination
Installed instruction files are written into target repositories under:

- `dev/instructions/`

Each installed module should use a predictable filename derived from the public module name.

### Public module naming
Public instruction modules use kebab-case names, such as:
- `development-governance`
- `parameterized-help`

These names are the values used in:
- `instructions_available()`
- `instructions_recipes()`
- `use_instructions()`

### Handler-based installation model
Each public instruction module has a corresponding handler function named:

- `module_<module_name>()`

where the public kebab-case module name is converted to snake_case for the function name.

Examples:
- `development-governance` -> `module_development_governance()`
- `parameterized-help` -> `module_parameterized_help()`

Each handler has two responsibilities:
1. install the module’s canonical static instruction text into `dev/instructions/`
2. perform any module-specific configuration required to support that module in the target repository

If no additional configuration is needed, the configuration portion of the handler is a no-op, but the handler still exists.

### Orchestration model
`use_instructions()` remains the main public entry point for instruction installation.

Its role is to:
- accept module names or recipe outputs
- normalize and validate requested modules
- resolve handler functions
- call handlers in order
- aggregate and summarize results

`use_instructions()` should remain a generic orchestrator. Module-specific setup logic belongs in the module handlers, not in `use_instructions()` itself.

### Module categories
The architecture supports two practical categories of modules.

#### Simple handlers
These modules install static instruction text and return a standard result object, but do not currently perform additional configuration.

Examples may include:
- `chat-manual`
- `goals`
- `r-package`

#### Config-aware handlers
These modules install static instruction text and also scaffold or validate repository structures needed to support the module.

Current or planned examples include:
- `development-governance`
- `parameterized-help`

### Result object model
Module handlers should return a standard structured result object that reports:
- module name
- instruction source path
- instruction target path
- directories created
- files written
- files skipped
- warnings
- next steps

This structure allows `use_instructions()` to aggregate module results and present a concise installation summary.

### Internal helper layer
The handler architecture is supported by internal helper functions for:
- module source path resolution
- module target path resolution
- directory creation
- static instruction installation
- module name to handler resolution
- result construction
- result aggregation
- module normalization and validation

These helpers exist to keep:
- module handlers small
- behavior consistent
- orchestration logic simple

## Development-governance capability

### Purpose
`reproducibleai` standardizes a development-governance framework for AI-assisted repositories that use iterative chat sessions as part of normal development work.

The goal is to make important outcomes from chat sessions durable, concise, and reviewable by promoting them into structured repository artifacts rather than leaving them only in archived transcripts.

### Governance model
The package assumes that internal development-state artifacts live in a repository `dev/` directory, while user-facing package documentation is communicated through standard package surfaces such as:
- `README`
- roxygen/man pages
- vignettes/articles
- pkgdown site pages

### Standard internal artifacts
The governance framework centers on these required artifacts:

- `dev/05_plan.md` for active work planning
- `dev/10_design.md` for stable current-state architecture and design
- `dev/40_schemas.md` for explicit structural and data contracts
- `dev/decisions/` for decision records
- `dev/instructions/` for modular chat/developer guidance
- `dev/sessions/` for archived transcripts

`dev/40_schemas.md` is required,

*System: User retried message*

## Copilot

Yes — the current `dev/10_design.md` is a good conceptual start, but it now needs to be updated to reflect the architecture decisions we clarified:

- `reproducibleai` remains **instruction-first**
- canonical instruction text remains static in `inst/instructions/`
- every module now has a corresponding handler function
- `use_instructions()` becomes an orchestrator over handlers
- `dev/40_schemas.md` is **required**, not optional
- development-governance support is shifting from standalone scaffolding functions toward **module-backed installation/configuration**
- parameterized-help should be framed as a later config-aware module under the same architecture

Below is a **paste-ready replacement** for `dev/10_design.md` that synthesizes the current contents with those newer decisions. It preserves your existing capability sections, but updates them to match the handler-based refactor and the stronger schema stance.

````markdown name=dev/10_design.md url=https://github.com/MVR-GIS/reproducibleai/blob/079792f7d02e00ab084f3cbb8a146013041d5ff0/dev/10_design.md
# Design

Last updated: 2026-05-30

## Purpose
This document records the current stable architecture, operating assumptions, and capability boundaries for `reproducibleai`.

The package is intended to support reproducible AI-assisted development workflows by standardizing:
- modular chat instructions
- recipe-based instruction composition
- transparent deployment of reviewed instruction text into target repositories
- lightweight configuration support where specific modules require supporting repository structure

## Document map
- Plan: `dev/05_plan.md`
- Schemas: `dev/40_schemas.md`
- Decisions: `dev/decisions/`
- Instructions: `dev/instructions/`
- Sessions: `dev/sessions/`

## Core architecture

### Instruction-first model
`reproducibleai` remains instruction-first.

The primary user-facing workflow is still:
1. discover available instruction modules
2. compose modules into a recipe
3. install them into a target repository with `use_instructions()`

This design is intentional. The package prioritizes:
- natural-language instruction definitions
- easy review by non-programmers
- simple UX through recipe composition
- visible, versioned static instruction text
- consistency across team repositories
- low conceptual complexity in a new workflow domain

### Static canonical instruction text
Canonical instruction module text is stored as static markdown in:

- `inst/instructions/`

These files are the authoritative reviewed instruction contents. They are intended to remain:
- easy to diff in Git
- readable by non-programmers
- stable across installations
- consistent across repositories using the same package version

Handlers and orchestration code must not rewrite or dynamically customize the substantive wording of these canonical instruction files.

### Handler-based installation model
Each public instruction module has a corresponding handler function named:

- `module_<module_name>()`

where:
- the public module name remains kebab-case
- the handler function name uses snake_case

Examples:
- `development-governance` -> `module_development_governance()`
- `parameterized-help` -> `module_parameterized_help()`

Each handler has two responsibilities:
1. copy the module’s canonical static instruction text from `inst/instructions/` into the target repository’s `dev/instructions/`
2. perform any module-specific repository configuration needed to support use of that module

If a module has no configuration needs, the configuration step is a no-op, but the handler still exists.

This provides one uniform execution model without giving up the static, reviewable instruction architecture.

### `use_instructions()` orchestration role
`use_instructions()` remains the main public entry point for installing instructions.

Its role is to:
- accept module names or recipe outputs
- normalize and validate requested modules
- resolve module handlers
- invoke handlers in order
- aggregate and summarize results

`use_instructions()` should not contain module-specific setup logic beyond generic orchestration. Module-specific behavior belongs in the corresponding handler.

### Module categories
The current architecture supports two practical categories of modules.

#### Simple handlers
These modules:
- install static instruction text
- return a standard result object
- perform no additional repo configuration

Examples include:
- `chat-manual`
- `goals`
- `r-package`
- `python-package`
- `quarto-book`
- `user-manual`

#### Config-aware handlers
These modules:
- install static instruction text
- scaffold or validate supporting repository structure
- return a standard result object that records installation and configuration actions

The first config-aware module is:
- `development-governance`

A later config-aware module is expected for:
- `parameterized-help`

## Development-governance capability

### Purpose
`reproducibleai` standardizes a development-governance framework for AI-assisted repositories that use iterative chat sessions as part of normal development work.

The goal is to make important outcomes from chat sessions durable, concise, and reviewable by promoting them into structured repository artifacts rather than leaving them only in archived session transcripts.

### Governance model
The package assumes that internal development-state artifacts live in a repository `dev/` directory, while user-facing package documentation is communicated through standard package surfaces such as:
- `README`
- roxygen/man pages
- vignettes/articles
- pkgdown site pages

### Standard internal artifacts
The development-governance framework centers on these required artifacts:

- `dev/05_plan.md` for active work planning
- `dev/10_design.md` for stable current-state architecture and design
- `dev/40_schemas.md` for structural contracts and schema documentation
- `dev/decisions/` for decision records
- `dev/instructions/` for modular chat/developer guidance
- `dev/sessions/` for archived transcripts

`dev/40_schemas.md` is required, not optional. In AI-assisted data science repositories, undocumented schemas and implicit structural assumptions are a common source of fragile, buggy behavior. Structural contracts should therefore be documented explicitly.

### Behavioral enforcement
This capability is enforced primarily through instruction modules and chat behavior rather than through heavy automation of substantive document writing.

The intended workflow is:
1. install the development-governance instruction module
2. scaffold the governance structure in the repository through the module handler
3. use instruction modules to condition chat sessions to monitor for governance-update triggers
4. require the chat session to provide paste-ready updates to the relevant `dev/` artifacts when durable project state changes

### Handler support model
The `development-governance` module is the first config-aware module in the handler architecture.

Its handler is responsible for:
- installing canonical `development-governance` instruction text into `dev/instructions/`
- scaffolding the required governance artifacts in the target repository
- preserving existing files by default unless overwrite is explicitly requested

Its handler is not responsible for:
- generating repo-specific design content
- writing actual plans or ADRs for the user
- replacing the chat session’s role in producing paste-ready governance updates during real work

### Scope boundaries
This governance capability is distinct from domain-specific package functionality.

It governs:
- documentation roles
- document precedence
- session-to-document promotion
- durable design capture
- explicit schema documentation as part of development continuity

It does not govern:
- package-specific implementation logic
- user-facing instructional content except where governance boundaries must be clarified

### Package responsibilities
`reproducibleai` should support this capability through:
- canonical static instruction modules in `inst/instructions/`
- module handlers that install instructions and scaffold required supporting structure
- `use_instructions()` as the high-level orchestration entry point
- published package documentation that explains the governance framework to users

## Parameterized-help capability

### Purpose
`reproducibleai` standardizes a reusable pattern for contextual help in `golem`-based Shiny apps developed as R packages.

This capability is intended to help teams implement help systems that are:
- structured as package data
- composed from stable reusable records
- rendered through shared helper functions
- maintainable as app functionality evolves

### Scope
This capability governs:
- help-data architecture
- help-data storage and package-data conventions
- stable help IDs
- composition and rendering of contextual help
- help-specific CSS support
- integrity and drift review practices

It does not govern:
- app domain logic
- general Shiny architecture
- general package governance outside the help system

### Standard architecture
The package assumes a help-data workflow with:
- `data-raw/create_help_data.R` as the authoring script
- `data/help_data.rda` as the package dataset
- `R/help_data.R` as dataset documentation
- stable `id`, `title`, `summary`, and `detail` fields
- overview and granular help records composed into app surfaces

This capability also reinforces the importance of explicit schema documentation, since the help system itself defines a maintained structured data contract.

### Runtime helper model
The preferred model is for app packages to import reusable help helpers from `reproducibleai` rather than copying them into each app repository.

`reproducibleai` should provide the reusable runtime behavior, while app repositories should own:
- their help content
- their help composition choices
- their app-specific help wording

### Handler support model
The `parameterized-help` module is expected to become a config-aware module under the same handler architecture used by development-governance.

Its handler should be responsible for:
- installing canonical `parameterized-help` instruction text
- scaffolding the package-data help framework where appropriate
- supporting a consistent help-data architecture across applications

### Maintenance model
This capability assumes that help is part of the app’s maintained interface.

When app functionality changes, help should be reviewed for:
- missing IDs
- stale names
- stale formulas
- stale unit descriptions
- composition mismatches with current UI surfaces

## Design invariants
The following are current architectural invariants:

- Canonical instruction text remains static in `inst/instructions/`.
- Every public instruction module has a corresponding handler function.
- Handlers install static instruction text and may perform module-specific configuration.
- Handlers do not rewrite substantive canonical instruction text.
- `use_instructions()` remains the main public entry point.
- Recipes remain simple compositions of public module names.
- `dev/40_schemas.md` is a required development-governance artifact.
- Development-governance is the first config-aware module in the handler architecture.
- Parameterized-help is expected to become a later config-aware module.

## Open questions
- Finalize helper implementations and tests for the handler-based refactor.
- Decide whether `use_instructions()` should return its combined result visibly or invisibly.
- Decide how `instructions_available()` should validate consistency between canonical source files and implemented handlers.
````

## Key changes synthesized from the current file

- adds a **top-level architecture section** instead of only capability sections
- makes the **instruction-first** philosophy explicit
- records the new **static text + handler** model
- redefines `use_instructions()` as an **orchestrator**
- changes `dev/40_schemas.md` from **optional** to **required**
- clarifies that development-governance is now a **config-aware module**
- places parameterized-help within the same handler architecture
- adds explicit **design invariants**
